11 October 1995

Key Developments

FBIS Duma Election Report 11 Oct
WA1110015195

(FBIS Media Note] This is the fourth in a weekly series
of reports that FBIS will produce on the campaign for
the 17 December Russian Duma elections. It is based
soiely on Russian media reporting.

Recent Developments

Criminal List Prompts Debate on Deputies’ Immunity
Minister of Internal Affairs (MVD) Anatoliy Kulikov’s
announcement that he had given the Central Electoral
Commission (CEC) a list of 85 candidates under inves-
tigation for criminal activities (Moscow Radio, 7 Oc-
tober) has led deputies in the Stability faction of the
Duma to begin campaigning for a constitutional amend-
ment to lift Duma deputies’ immunity from prosecu-
tion (INTERFAX, 5 October). CEC Chairman Nikolay
Ryabov announced his backing for the faction’s efforts
and said that the CEC is “analyzing the lists of candi-
dates” and “exposing people who have some connection
with the criminal world.” Campaigning in Rostov, Rus-
sia’s Democratic Choice leader Yegor Gaydar said that
he also agrees that immunity should be lifted so that
the Duma does not “turn into a club of criminals hiding
from justice" (Kommersant-Daily, 5 October).

In a related matter, the Duma voted to recommend that
the Prosecutor General strip Sergey Mavrodi, head of
the well-known MMM pyramid scheme, of his deputy’s
seat. The vote was 303-0, three votes more than the two-
thirds majority required by Duma rules. Duma Mandate
Commission Chairman Vitaliy Sevastyanov said that
documents from the tax police and the State Taxation
Inspectorate indicate that Mavrodi still heads the MMM
Investment Fund and has set up 13 more branches
during his term in the Duma, thus violating a ban
on “commercial activities" by lawmakers (ITAR-TASS,
6 October). The CEC recently rejected the election
registration application for Mavrodi’s Party of People’s
Capital.

CEC Warns Against Breaking Campaign Rules At a
meeting on 5 October with representatives of several
parties and blocs, CEC Chairman Ryabov outlined the
legal guidelines for the Duma elections and reportedly
indicated that “offenders” will be “severely punished"
(Krasnaya Zvezda, 6 October). The Defense Ministry's
newspaper pointed out that "in most cases” such “of-
fenders" can “expect a rather long investigation, which
in effect means being out of the election struggle." Ac-
cording to Krasnaya Zvezda, “it could turn out that be-
cause of a single, shall we say, ‘hitch,’ several hun-
dred or even thousand signatures might simply not get

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES 1

counted toward the total of a particular party or in-
dependent candidate.” Such a “hitch” sidelined several
aspirants prior to the December 1993 Duma elections,
including the “Accord for the Sake of the Fatherland"
political movement headed by current Congress of Rus-
sian Communities (KROQ) leader Yuriy Skokov.

Shumeyko on Federation Council Support for Re-
gional Candidates Federation Council Chairman
Viadimir Shumeyko said at a 3 October news confer-
ence that “you will see decisions or actions on how the
Federation Council and its chairman personally” will
support regional candidates “once it is known which
election blocs" qualify for the ballot by the 22 October
deadline. Shumeyko asserted that members of the upper
chamber of parliament “will do our best" to support
regional candidates “who possess the required moral
and professional qualities, legislative and management
experience, and [who] represent the interests of regions
and are supported by people locally" (INTERFAX, 3
October). Earlier, Shumeyko publicly indicated that he
supports Sverdlovsk Oblast Governor Eduard Rossel’s
Transformation of the Fatherland bloc (see "FBIS
Duma Election Report 26 Sep”).

Kozyrev Running as an Independent in Murmansk
Foreign Minister Andrey Kozyrev told INTERFAX (6
October) he has submitted supporting signatures to
seek reelection in Murmansk’s Murmanskiy Electoral
District (Electoral District No. 116) as an “independent
candidate.” Although he is listed among the single-seat
catididates for Premier Viktor Chernomyrdin’s Russia
Is Our Home (NDR) bloc (Rossiyskaya Gazeta, 26
September), Kozyrev told INTERFAX that he is not
running on the NDR ticket because “I believe that I
will be more useful" to the Murmansk region as “an
independent who does not have to stick to a party line.”
While noting that he supports “numerous provisions” of
the NDR program, Kozyrev somewhat cryptically said
that “one does not change horses when one crosses a
river." An unidentified “source” in the “Kozyrev circle”
told INTERFAX that the foreign minister's decision to
run as an independent "has been coordinated” with “top”
Russian officials, including President Boris Yeltsin.
Kozyrev ran on the Russia’s Choice ticket in 1993 and
won election in the Murmansk single-seat race.

Should he win and decide to keep his Duma seat,
Kozyrev will not be allowed to continue as foreign
minister. The Constitution prevents Duma deputies fron
performing other "state service," although an exception
was made on a “transitional” basis for members of the
current Duma. According to the 7 October Segodnya,
however, Kozyrev suggested that the electoral law was
unclear on the issue, saying that he hopes that the issue
of holding dual posts will be "clarified" because he

is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

2 DUMA ELECTION ISSUES

“likes both jobs." Asked by INTERFAX if Kozyrev
would give up his ministerial post, the unidentified
“source” said that the foreign minister is “not a man
to deceive the hopes of his electors.”

Candidate Grachev? According to a report released by
Ekho Moskvy’s information agency (6 October), My
Fatherland leader and Grachev antagonist Gen. Boris
Gromov “announced” in an interview with one of the
independent radio station's correspondents that Defense
Minister Pavel Grachev “plans” to “stand personally"
for election to the Duma in one of Saratov Oblast’s
single-seat electoral districts. While Grachev has not
been observed to have publicly confirmed or denied
Gromov’s assertion, another Grachev foe, Duma deputy
and human rights activist Sergey Yushenkov, told Ekho
Moskvy that he “approves” of such an action on
Grachev’s part. Yushenkov said that he would "help"
Grachev get elected because “it would mean he would
not be defense minister" any longer and “then the entire
armed forces and the country as a whole would heave
a sigh of relief.”

In the Provinces

More Fallout for Military From Volgograd Elections
Despite the total failure of all military candidates in
the | October Volgograd city elections (see "FBIS
Duma Election Report 4 Oct), leaders in the region and
the local media have protested the military's conduct
during the local campaign, according to an ITAR-
TASS report (4 October). They sent a “letter” to
Grachev protesting the “aggressive activities" of the
Volgograd Military District command and claiming that
the military's actions resembled an “attempt to seize
power by force." The letter reportedly alleged that a
garrison commander had "authorized" servicemen to
“display military hardware and other ammunition,” hold
a parade, and use portable radio equipment as "a means
of psychological pressure to make the city’s population
vote for the military candidates." The letter was handed
over to Col. Gen. Anatoliy Bogdanov, the deputy chief
of the General Stati, who then met in Volgograd with
Mayor Yuriy Chekhov “behind closed doors” for two
hours to “clarify the matter," according to ITAR-TASS.

Tatarstan Candidates Appear on Two Party Lists
Izvestiya Tatarstan (5 October) reported that two local
candidates are apparently ignoring the Duma Election
Law’s prohibition against running on more than one
party’s slate. The president of the Tatarstan Union of
Writers, Rinat Mukhamadiyev, is number 5 on the fed-
eral candidate list of former Vice-President Aleksandr
Rutskoy’s Derzhava party and fifth on NDR’s Tatarstan
regional list. Sergey Shashurin, president of the "Kazan-
Tan" association, heads the regional lists and is a single-

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

seat district candidate for two parties—Derzhava and
eye surgeon Svyatoslav Fedorov 's Party of Workers’
Self-Government. Shashurin is running in Tatarstan’s
Privolzhskiy Electoral District (Electoral District No.
26), and, according to Izvestiya Tatarstan, he has al-
ready collected the required number of signatures to
appear on the ballot.

Izvestiya Tatarstan also reported that the CPRF’s (Com-
munist Party of the Russian Federation) first secretary
in Tatarstan, Aleksandr Saliya, is the only other can-
didate in the districi to collect the necessary signatures
as of 4 October. Other aspiring candidates in the dis-
trict include Federal Duma Deputy Valentin Mikhaylov
on the Russia’s Democratic Choice-United Democrats
ticket, and NDR’s Aleksey Kolesnik, a deputy in the
republic’s legislature.

NOR Defector Claims Bloc Using Strong-Arm Tactics
Aleksandr Kashcheyev, who until recently was deputy
chairman of Stavropol Kray’s NDR branch, said at a 5
October news conference that he had been “threatened”
by NDR “activists” after breaking with the bloc and
“hampered” from collecting supporting signatures in his
effort to run as a single-seat candidate. Kashcheyev also
claimed NDR officials had sent down a “written instruc-
tion” to regional leaders to launch signature-collection
campaigns in support of the bloc and that local indus-
trial managers were having their arms twisted" by NDR
Officials to “try to make them comply with the instruc-
tion” (INTERFAX, 5 October). In his account of the
bloc’s early September congress at which the list of
NDR candidates was named, Kashcheyev claimed that
NDR Executive Committee Chairman Leonard Vid had
drawn up a “secret instruction" on how to vote on the
candidate lists and that input from regional delegates
was ignored. Kashcheyev’s remarks were reported in
Novaya Yezhednevnaya Gazeta, No. 33, 7-13 Septem-
ber.

Stavropol Polls’ Results Contradictory Two polls of
potential Stavropol voters appear to have yielded con-
flicting results. According to the 30 September Sev-
ernyy Kavkaz, a poll by Moscow “sociologists” showed
Women of Russia having the highest approval rating
among various parties, with a 45.6 perceni rating. The
paper reported that the CPRF finished “second” (al-
though it apparently listed erroneously the party's rating
as 6.7), followed by the Agrarian Party of Russia (21.1),
NDR (20), Russia’s Democratic Choice (5.6), and the
KRO, which finished with a rating of “absolutely zero.”

A separate poll by the local Center for Humanitarian
Research came up with entirely different results, how-
ever. Yabloko had the largest number of sympathizers
(no figures given), followed in order by the CPRF, Rus-

sia’s Democratic Choice, NDR, the Liberal Democratic
Party of Russia, and Women of Russia.

Taganrog Communists Split The Taganrog city branch
of the CPRF has reportedly split over selection of a
candidate to run in the local district. Severnyy Kavkaz
reported on 30 September that the local party’s "most
fiery orator" and “organizer of all of the communists’
local legislative activity," party second secretary Oleg
Nabokov, “deserted” the party and is running as a can-
didate for former Soviet Premier Nikolay Ryzhkov’s
Power to the People! party in Rostov Oblast’s Tagan-
rogskiy Electoral District (Electoral District No. 147).
He reportedly expected to be the CPRF’s candidate to
challenge Gen. Yuriy Rodionov, who narrowly defeated
Nabokov in the 1993 Duma elections. The party, how-
ever, selected the city branch’s first secretary, Niko-
lay Borisenko, as its candidate, prompting Nabokov and
other “comrades” to leave the party

Other candidates for the seat include Duma deputy and
NDR candidate Sergey Shapovalov; head of the Rostov
Obiast office of the Federal Security Service (FSB),
Sergey Mironov, from Yabloko’s list; and director of
the “Puls” television-radio company, Aleksey Sklyarov,
from Russia’s Democratic Choice.

Recently Released Businesswoman Running in
Podolsk Valentina Solovyeva, released from jail on
30 September after serving a three-month term for
tax evasion and fraud, has reportedly collected the
required number of signatures to support her candi-
dacy in Moscow Oblast’s Podolskiy Electoral District
(Electoral District No. 112). INTERFAX reported on
2 October that Solovyeva, owner of the "Vlastilina"
firm, has collected over 5,000 signatures, with 4,461
Signatures required for a candidate to appear on the
ballot in the district. Moskovskiye Novosti had reported
in its 24 September-1 October issue that most of the
signatures came from military units near Moscow,
where many officers reportedly invested in her firm and
are still hoping to see a return on these investments.
INTERFAX quoted sources from the district’s electoral
commission as saying that her lists of signatures in-
clude “major violations of the election law," including
“missing passport numbers” and names and information
that were alleged to have been rewritten after signatures
were collected.

Solovyeva’s case took on added significance in
September when Duma Security Committee Chairman
Viktor Ilyukhin asserted that during her testimony,
Solovyeva accused Federation Council Chairman
Vladimir Shumeyko and First Deputy Premier Oleg
Soskovets of having “directly or through figureheads”
borrowed “many millions and billions" of rubles from

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES 3

“Vlastilina," “gaining enormous profits" (/zvestiya,
15 September). Shumeyko and Soskovets denied the
accusations, and Moskovskiye Novosti contended that
Ilyukhin himself had invested $700,000 in the firm,
displaying a copy of his alleged investment certificate.

The Media Beat

ORT To Aid All "Sensible" Political Forces Public
Russian Television (ORT) General Director Sergey
Blagovolin told RIA (7 October) that ORT will air all
campaign programs "strictly in accordance” with CEC
guidelines. He added, however, that ORT’s leadership
will have the final say about “all other social and
political programs, some of which may deal with the
election campaign" and will “help all sensible political
forces." According to Blagovolin, ORT’s "policy" is
to give support to “all political forces that advocate
national harmony, stability, and a search for a sensible
compromise that will make it possible to keep Russia
together and continue reforms.”

4 DUMA ELECTION ISSUES

Election Schedule—Key Dates

22 October: Deadline for submitting 200,000 signatures
in support of party lists, with no more than 7 percent
coming from a single republic or region. Single-seat
candidates must collect signatures equaling at least 1
percent of a district’s voters (Federal electoral law
published in the 28 June Rossiyskaya Gazeta). Although
22 October is also the deadline for registering party
lists and single-seat nominations, parties and candidates
cannot begin collecting signatures until after they are
registered. Registration of party and single-seat lists
began after the list of electoral districts was published
in Rossiyskaya Gazeta on 22 August.

5 November: Parties and candidates can begin running
campaign advertisements in state-owned federal and
regional newspapers (ITAR-TASS, 20 September).

15 November-15 December: Equal and free access for
airtime on state-owned federal and regional television
and radio channels granted for all candidates; 30-minute
blocs of free airtime from 7:00-10:00 and 18:00-23:30
to be drawn by lots.

On a Lighter Note...

The Problem is Not With Your TV Set... Apparently
reacting to the frequent television appearances of ro-
tund Russia’s Democratic Choice leader Yegor Gay-
dar, Derzhava leader and former Vice-President Alek-
sandr Rutskoy reportedly said at a press conference in
Krasnodar that he has "an enormous tape measure” on
his television set that he uses "to measure Gaydar’s
face.” Adding another sarcastic note about the presi-
dential election scheduled for June 1996, Rutskoy said
that his "big parrot"—which he claimed knows “only
250 words"—could be “elected president of Russia”
(Izvestiya, 4 October).

Sverdlovsk Governor Rossel Voices Support for
Yeltsin

MK1010065095 Moscow SEGODNYA in Russian
6 Oct 95 p2

[Report by Yelena Tregubova: "Eduard Rossel Will
Support "‘Only Boris Yeltsin.’ The Governor Does Not
Fear the Results of Elections for the Duma as Long as
the Country Has Such a President")

[FBIS Translated Text] Eduard Rossel, recently-elected
governor of Sverdlovsk Oblast, assured journalists [in
Moscow] yesterday that he would support "Boris Niko-
layevich alone” in the presidential elections. Responding
to a question on whether he is "on Yeltsin’s team,” Mr.
Rossel suggested that the journalists "proceed from the
fact that always, even at the most difficult moments,”
he "supported Boris Nikolayevich, although I had colos-

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

sal troubles over that." "On two occasions during my
life, I received cables with a direct threat of physical
violence for supporting Yeltsin,” said Mr. Rossel. “The
first was sent to me by Yanayev at 0200 during the well-
known days of 1991. He threatened capital punishment
if I carried out Boris Yeltsin's edicts. The second was
sent by Rutskoy, also at 0200, but in 1993. The content
was precisely the same, so much so that I even thought
those people had prepared the text once and for all, only
putting different signatures under it.”

Prime Minister Chernomyrdin received far fewer
warm words from Eduard Rossel than the president.
The Sverdlovsk governor predicted that Viktor Cher-
nomyrdin will soon either ask to be discharged or the
president will discharge him.” Mr. Rossel vouched this
would be retribution for the fact that “with a living
president,” Viktor Chernomyrdin dared to create his
own political movement. True, Mr. Governor could not
very well explain how such predictions mesh with the
president's direct “blessing” of the NDR [Russia Is Our
Home] and Boris Yeltsin's repeated statements on “full
understanding with my premier.”

Most probable, however, is that yesterday's criticism
of the premier is no more than an inalienable part of
the electoral campaign of Eduard Rossel, whose party
("Transformation of the Homeland”) was conceived as
an “organization of regional politicians independent of
the center” — the more so because, as the Sverdlovsk
governor admitted yesterday, he “read the rules of
“Russia Is Our Home’ in detail and from cover to cover"
and “did not like” these rules.

Eduard Rossel admitted that he generally does not think
the parliamentary elections “decisive for the country.”
“Even if all the seats in the Duma are taken by
Communists, they will pose no danger because property
has been almost reformed, ard the country has a reliable
guarantor of stability — the president, who would not
have the country being mocked. The communists would
willy-nilly have to begin to cooperate constructively,
otherwise the president would put up with such a Duma
for six months and then dissolve it,” the governor stated.

Chernomyrdin: Elections Not To Be Postponed or
Canceled

LDO910205295 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1951 GMT 9 Oct 95

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, Oct 9 (INTER-
FAX) — Russian Prime Minister Viktor Chernomyrdin
on Monday promised that the next parliamentary and
presidential elections would be neither canceled nor
postponed and predicted that the country’s economy

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

would undergo “radical” positive changes before the end
of the century.

The parliamentary election would be held on December
17, the presidential election next June, as scheduled,
Chernomyrdin assured a group of U.S. business people
and the senior editors of the Time magazine, whom he
received in Moscow.

He expressed the belief that the main result of the
elections would be a firmer policy of market reforms.
“The reforms have become irreversible, and Russia will
not divert from the market path,” he said.

There is “a lot of work" for the government to do
before the parliamentary election, the prime minister
continued. He said his government had, to date, drafted
and submitted to parliament more than 80 laws to
improve economic mechanisms.

“We hope that the future parliament will work more
effectively than the present one,” Chernomyrdin said.

Speaking in this connection about Russia’s development
prospects, the premier said the country “will carry
through a radical change in the economy” before the
end of the century.

Text of Commission Chairmen’s Election Appeal

MM1010124295 Moscow ROSSIYSKIYE VESTI
in Russian 7 Oct 95 p 3

{"Appeal to Participants in the Election Campaign for
the Election of Deputies to the Russian Federation Fed-
eral Assembly State Duma, Second Convocation" from
“participants in a seminar-conference of chairmen of
electoral commissions of the Russian Federation compo-
nents and district electoral commissions for the Russian
Federation Federal Assembly State Duma elections";
date not given]

{FBIS Translated Text] Moscow — Esteemed Fellow
Citizens!

The chairmen of the electoral commissions of Russian
Federation components and electoral district commis-
sions for the Russian Federation Federal Assembly State
Duma elections in the Central, Central Chernozem, and
Northwest regions and the North Caucasus appeal to
you.

Russians” attention is currently focused on the upcom-
ing elections of 17 December 1995. This is understand-
able: On the results of these elections will in many ways
depend the achievement of political and socioeconomic
stability in our society, the fate of the reforms, people's
standard of living, and Russia’s future path of develop-
ment.

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES 5

The election campaign, as the year’s most important
political event, has provoked an unprecedented growth
in the activity of parties and public associations: More
than 30 have joined the battle for seats in parliament
independently, and more than 50 as part of election
blocs. The federal lists of candidates already include
more than 14,000 names. The average number of
candidates contesting each seat in the State Duma
in single-seat districts in the country is 20, and this
includes only those nominated by electoral associations
and election blocs.

Fully aware of the responsibility laid on us by the law,
we appeal to Russia’s citizens and to participants in the
election campaign: .

We appeal to all voters: Russia’s future is in your
hands. Play an active part in the forthcoming elections.
In this critical time there should be no place for
indifference or pessimism, any more than for excessive
hotheadedness. Make a deliberate choice, based on an
objective assessment of the policy documents of the
election associations and the merits of the candidates;

We appeal to young people: The country’s future in
many ways depends on whom young people vote for;

We appeal to election associations and electoral blocs:
Show the maximum restraint during the election cam-
paign, behave honorably and with dignity, uphold the
spirit and letter of the law steadfastly;

We appeal directly to those who have entered the
campaign for parliamentary seats: Your rivalry should
not sow the seeds of discord in society. Do not allow
the character of your relations with your opponents to
be clouded by unethical attacks and unjustified actions;

We appeal to representatives of the mass media: Be
objective and dispassionate. The abuse of the voters’
trust in television broadcasts and newspaper articles is
unacceptable. The possibilities of television, radio, and
the press should be used only for the good of society
and only within the framework of the law;

We appeal to leaders of the executive and legislative
organs of state power at all levels: Mutual, constructive
cooperation based on the norms of the law is the
essential condition for ensuring the democratic course
of the election campaign;

We appeal to the law-enforcement organs, urgently
requesting that you enhance your attention to questions
connected with the observance of legislation at the
elections. Do not allow violations of the electoral rights
of Russian Federation citizens and other participants in
the electoral process;

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

6 DUMA ELECTION ISSUES

We appeal to our fellow members of electoral commis-
sions at all levels: Our joint actions should be subordi-
nated to our main task of ensuring steadfast observance
of the law and fair, free, and democratic elections. Open-
ness and glasnost in our work should be the indispens-
able condition in carrying out all election activity.

We are convinced that our appeal will meet with
understanding and that our common actions will serve
the good of Russia.

CEC Grants CPRF R80 Million for Election
Campaign

LD0910212295 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1951 GMT 9 Oct 95

(FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, Oct 9 (INTER-
FAX) — The Russian Central Electoral Commission
[CEC] will extend 80 million rubles [R] for the financ-
ing of the election campaign of the first registered elec-
toral bloc: the Communist Party of Russia [CPRF], as
provided for by the law on parliamentary elections.

The Central Election Commission made such a decision
today.

Under the law, the Central Election Commission will
earmark equal sums of money for the election campaign
of every registered electoral bloc or movement.

Candidates to the State Duma, electoral blocs, move-
ments and parties also form their own election funds
for financing their election campaign.

In line with legislation, the Central Election Commis-
sion has permitted the Communist Party to open a spe-
cial temporary account in the Moscow bank of the Rus-
sian SberBank for forming the election fund.

The Central Election Commission today also certi-
fied the lists of candidates to the State Duma to
be fielded in single-mandate constituencies by the
National-Republican Party of Russia (4 people) and the
Independents’ Bloc (109 people).

UCP-CPSU Chairman Corrects Article on CPRF
Registration

MM1010114095 Moscow PRAVDA in Russian

7 Oct 95 p2

[PRAVDA "Correction"]

{(FBIS Translated Text] The PRAVDA editorial office
has received a letter from O. Shenin, chairman of the
UCP-CPSU [Union of Communist Parties-Communist
Party of the Soviet Union] Council. In it he notes that an
error was made in PRAVDA’s item “Communists Join
Forces After All” (3 October this year). In particular, the
appeal supporting the CPRF election bloc was adopted
not at a session of the UCP-CPSU Political Executive
Committee but at a working conference of secretaries
of former Union republics’ communist parties.

The author of the item and the PRAVDA editorial office
apologize to readers.

Regional Activities
Zorin: Chechnya Preparing for State Duma
Elections

LD0810201195 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
1941 GMT 8 Oct 95

[By Sergey Ostanin]

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] GROZNYY October 8 (ITAR-
TASS) — The election to the Russian State Duma,
or the lower house, to be held on December 17 is in
preparation in Chechnya, Vladimir Zorin, acting chief
of the territorial federal executive administration in
Chechnya, told reporters today.

He said a total of 35 parties and public groups had
registered for the election and started to pool in electoral
blocs.

Struggle for electorates is already in progress, although
it is still much debated whether Chechnya should take
part in the State Duma election, Zorin said.

He voiced confidence that Chechnya’s candidates would
run for the State Duma.

“We may not refuse (this) to people who want to run in
the election," Zorin said.

He said six candidates are being nominated in Chechnya
as a territorial constituency and seven-eight candidates
are to run on territorial lists on parties and groups.

Local rule should be elected in Chechnya simultane-
ously with the State Duma election, Zorin said, adding
that a key prerequisite for this is the laying down of
arms by rebel factions and the population.

Communists Better Organized Than Democrats in
Amur Oblast

MMI1010092995 Moscow ROSSIYSKIYE VESTI
in Russian 6 Oct 95 p2

{Report by Nikolay Belyy under the “Elections-95"
rubric: "While the Democrats Squabble, the Commu-
nists Are Having a Field Day"]

[FBIS Translated Text] Blagoveshchensk — The elec-
tion campaign has even gripped the distant regions of
Russia. Away from the capital, a large number of peo-
ple wanting to take up their own seats in the Moscow’s
Duma have also suddenly emerged.

In Chita Oblast, some 15 candidates are vying for one
Statutory seat in the Russian Parliament, while there
are just as many in neighboring Amur Oblast. Military
personnel of various ranks, civilians from a whole range
of mainly official occupations — everyone has suddenly
wanted to devote themselves to the noble pursuit of
composing state laws for their compatriots to live by in
the future. Parliamentarians, who are already prospering
and who, it seems, have taken a liking to the easy
and nowadays lucrative work in the State Duma, have
appeared on the lists of candidates. They want to render
still more service to the poor Russian people.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

18 DUMA ELECTION ISSUES

To all appearances, the Duma deputies from Amur
Oblast, who had in the past, or who are currently
being put forward by the communist party, will have
no difficulty in fulfilling their intention of remaining
under the roof of Russia’s supreme legislative assembly
for a second term. First, the situation in the provinces,
first and foremost the economic situation, is working in
their favor. Second, the heirs of “honor and conscience”
are much better organized and more determined than
their political opponents. In alliance with the agrarians
and trade unions, the communists nominated a single
candidate for the single-seat 58th Blagoveshchensk
electoral district and placed obedient old men and
women and ideologically sound activists in all corners
both of Amur Oblast’s capital and outlying areas.
Their efforts to collect signatures are flourishing. As
the regional communist party branch so emotionally
informed me, it has almost finished collecting signatures
both in support of the “party,” thai is for the federal list
of candidates of the Communist Party of the Russian
Federation, and in support of the local candidate. The
communists even collect spare votes in case of an
emergency with ease.

The situation is completely the opposite for the
democrats. A single candidate, to suit all the whole
assortment of directions within the region’s democratic
movement, has not been found. Within the camp of the
oblast branch of "Russia’s Democratic Choice" scores
are being settled, public statements are being made
about members leaving, and accusations of corruption
and some or other dirty tricks are being made. It even
turns out that things are being thieved from the tiny
cash office of the small regional branch of “Russia’s
Democratic Choice." The search for the miscreants
is being passed off as a struggle to purge the party
ranks. The man on the street is, however, simply
shocked by the internal party squabbles, which have,
moreover, started when an election campaign is in
full swing. Even with his passport, he cannot get into
the place to which the democrats are inviting him to
go. The headquarters of the Amur Oblast branch of
“Russia’s Democratic Choice” is deserted. The office’s
sole occupier says that the collection of signatures "in
support of Gaydar" is proceeding with difficulty and
that the results even of the election race’s first leg are
difficult to predict.

Moreover, spokes are, as always, being put in the
democrats’ wheel. Supporters of Gaydar in Amur Oblast
set up their premises in the Blagoveshchensk tax po-
lice’s building, renting a room there. A burly guard in-
side the police building barred the way to me, having
demanded my papers and interrogated me about the pur-
pose of my visit. If you do not have the documentation,

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

you Cannot get into the public organization which calls
itself a people’s organization, even with an ID card.
One luckless supporter of “Russia’s Democratic Choice"
phoned the ROSSIYSKIYE VESTI office and reported
that the guard at the [tax] police [premises] did not let
him in to see the democrats even with his passport, hav-
ing showed him a note on which was written in a clear
hand: "Visitors not to be admitted to office 315!"

At my question about who had issued the censorial
political directive, the uniformed guard shrugged his
shoulders. The note itself disappeared as documentary
evidence. Meantime, here in the police building, not
far from the vigilant guards, an old man and supporter
of Zyuganov collected signatures in support of the
communist party and its resolute candidates. No passes
were demanded in order to get to see him him.

Daily Examines Election Prospects of CPRF

MM1010113895 Moscow SOVETSKAYA ROSSIYA
in Russian 7 Oct 95 pS

{Article by Doctor of Historical Sciences Sergey
Vasiltsov and Candidate of Economic Sciences Sergey
Obukhov of the Center for the Study of Russian
Political Values: "The Communist Party of the Russian
Federation’s Voters: The ‘Communist Threat’ Since
the ‘Burial of Communism’ — first paragraph is
SOVETSKAYA ROSSIYA introduction}

{[FBIS Translated Excerpt] Recently Andrey Loginov,
one of the president’s chief analysts, rebuked the mass
media: "By hyping the theme of communist revenge,” he
Stated agitatedly, “political commentators and journal-
ists are — wittingly or otherwise — disorienting voters
and thereby indirectly helping to increase the political
weight of procommunist electoral associations." As if
Carrying out instructions "from above,” one of the capi-
tal’s pro-government research foundations immediately
reported its poll findings: Communists are supported by
no more than 10 percent of voters. However, the recent
local elections in Volgograd — former bailiwick of the
radical democrats — clearly showed the tunnel vision of
the official pollsters. Therefore, the editorial office again
asked the specialists of the independent Center for the
Study of Russian Political Values to provide its analysis
of the development of mass attitudes in Russia and the
changes in the position held by the Communist Party of
the Russian Federation [CPRF] among the Russiawide
electorate. [passage omitted]

The last two years (1993 through 1995) have seen
the voters of all the other parties devastated. All of
them, that is, save the CPRF. More than 80 percent

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

of the CPRF’s former voters confirm their loyalty. The
CPRF has conceded virtually nothing to other electoral
associations. At the same time one-fourth of the former
supporters of the Liberal Democratic Party of Russia,
9 percent of former supporters of Yabloko, 13 percent
of the old Russia’s Choice electorate, and 18 percent
of former Agrarian Party of Russia voters stated their
preference for the Communists.

But this is n0 way means the automatic conversion of
former supporters of other parties into real voters.

According to a poll carried out by the center in
September, 18-20 percent of the national electorate are
ready to vote for the CPRF. The backbone — around
three-fourths of them — are “veterans” of the 1993
election. One-tenth is made up of those who did not vote
in 1993. The remainder are former supporters of other
parties, but there are fewer of them now than during a
poll carried out at the start of the summer.

The poll data of that period show that 5 percent more
voters were ready to vote for the CPRF at that time

DUMA ELECTION ISSUES 19

than now. Of course, measurements of voting intentions
carried out earlier also showed large fluctuations in
support for the CPRF during the year. Yes, the party
can bank on its 1993 voters, but they are not enough
to ensure the electoral breakthrough which is being
prophesied for Zyuganov’s party from all sides. The
campaign to win over other parties’ voters and floating
voters will clearly be difficult.

Let us remember what happens when you celebrate
victory prematurely. You get the breakup of Russia’s
Choice, which turned the "political New Year" into a
fiasco. The same thing happened to the Chernomyrdin
bloc, which, having peaked too soon, is now on the
verge of collapse. And something akin — congratula-
tions on a victory not yet won — is happening with
regard to the CPRF. And yet we are only observing so-
ciety at the stage before the election race begins, and
another two event-packed months remain before the fin-
ishing straight is entered.

20 INTERREGIONAL AFFAIRS

Danger, Impact of Loss of CIS Markets Weighed

MM1110111695 Moscow ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA
in Russian 7 Oct 95 pp 9, 10

{Article by Vladimir Kucherenko: "The Near Abroad
Must Not Become the Far Abroad"]}

(FBIS Translated Text] The Russian Government is
making efforts to restore its lost ties with the CIS
countries. To that end targeted, tied credits of 200 billion
rubles [R] have been included in the 1996 budget. But
these funds, apparently, satisfy few people.

Breaking Up Was Easy

Russia has failed to become a full-fledged link in the
international market — not a raw materials-producing
appendage of the developed West but a supplier of
finished output. In these conditions the CIS remains
almost the only market for the sale of our machine
building and high-technology output.

Meanwhile, Russia remains dependent on the import
of nonferrous metals, rare-earth elements, cotton, and
food from the CIS. At the same time our neighbors’
production is dependent on the export of energy sources
from the Russian Federation.

Despite all that, however, the highly unfavorable trends
in the Russian Federation’s trade and economic ties with
the CIS countries are becoming stronger. Reciprocal
trade volumes are falling.

Of course, the decline in production in all countries of
the ex- USSR plays its part. Overall CIS GDP fell by
39 percent in 1992-1994, although the decline in GDP
in Russia (25 percent in 1992-1994) noticeably lagged
behind the fall in commodity turnover with the CIS
neighbors (almost half).

Energy sources are playing an ever-increasing role in
the Russia’s trade with the CIS, rising from one-third
of the 1992 volume to one-half in 1994. In turn, Russia’s
imports increasingly consist of food and food raw
materials.

Meanwhile, Russia has been charged with the "duty"
of financing its neighbors’ independence. It is no
exaggeration to say that the Russian Federation is the
major sponsor of the "new abroad.” The CIS countries’
debts to Russia for Russian credits in 1992-1994, which
have now become state debts, have reached $5.8 billion
(Ukraine — R9.7 trillion; Belarus — R2.2 trillion;
Kazakhstan — R1.4 trillion; Moldova — R1.2 trillion).

To the huge debt for energy sources you have to add
afrears of R1.6 trillion on direct economic contracts.
The enormous debts of Ukraine and Kazakhstan have
placed “Roskontrakt" in a difficult position. However,

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

those CIS countries’ debts to the developed countries
are 2.4 times higher than their debts to Russia.

Experts from the Ministry for Cooperation with
Member-States of the CIS noted bitterly that the CIS
countries are reorienting themselves toward the far
abroad. Russia has ceased to be the technological and
financial center of the CIS. The developed countries are
actively trying to force Russia out of its traditional sales
markets. It may be boldly asserted that the Russian
Federation has generously funded the first two years
of the new countries’ independence but that the latter
are now actively switching to ties exclusively with the
"far" abroad.

Thus Russia is also being deprived of real levers
of influence over the noneconomic processes in the
CIS, and this is fraught with unpleasant surprises of
a geopolitical and strategic defense character for the
Russian Federation.

All this is very unpleasant for Russia. For example,
despite the fact that 80 percent of the military industry is
concentrated here, the Russian Federation can produce
only 17 types of arms and military hardware without
regular supplies of components from the CIS. With the
further alienation of the former republics of the USSR
the army runs the risk of being left with unreliable
outmoded weapons which will constantly break down.
It is hard to manage without cotton and food too.

What is the solution to this situation?

Fuel and Energy Factors

Let us address a specific question — the trade in
energy resources. The time for the primitive cry “Let’s
sell our oil to the West, we'll manage without the
Ukrainians and the Uzbeks!" has gone. The prices at
which the CIS countries buy energy sources from us
are rapidly catching up with the world level, and this
has boomeranged back on Russia by disrupting the
manufacturing chains and causing a further decline in
our production.

On the other hand, the CIS countries have begun fren-
ziedly seeking their own energy reserves and switched
their attention to other producer states.

The Russian Federation Ministry for Cooperation with
Member-States of the CIS believes that on this issue
the aim must be not only to obtain money for energy
supplies but also to switch to joint programs in the
fuel and energy complex. That is especially important
now that the Russian fuel and energy complex has
been hit by an investment crisis, with capacities being
decommissioned three to four times faster than new ones
are being commissioned.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

That is why it is advisable to draw up a common energy
resource supply program for the CIS while working as
actively as possible to involve our neighbors in fuel
and energy projects in Russia and ensuring the greatest
possible participation for the Russian Federation in
projects on CIS territory, such as in the Caspian
consortium.

The creation of transnational financial-industrial groups
would considerably ease the problem (we will talk
about the difficulties in this regard later). Enterprises
of this kind could receive local assets of the CIS states
in payment of their debts to Russia. This process got
under way with the agreement between the "Gazprom"
Russian Joint-Stock Company and Ukraine (20 March
1995) and the Russia-Moldova protocol on the payment
of "gas" debts signed two days later.

Let us note that the Russian Federation Committee for
the Chemical and Petrochemical Industry tried to "force
through" a similar model in trade with the CIS although
‘t was keenly opposed by Russian Federation Economics
Ministry leaders Ye. Yasin and Ya. Urinson.

There is another way to settle accounts for Russian
energy systems — the construction of housing for the
West Siberian oil and gas complex. That model is due to
be tried out with Belarus. This could include the supply
of provisions and industrial goods to the Russian North.

Financial-Industrial Groups — More on Paper at
the Moment

For almost three years now there has been talk about
another form of mutually beneficial cooperation —
financial-industrial groups — whereby enterprises from
various republics could join forces and pool their raw
materials, finances, and technology which are currently
divided by the new borders.

However, there are counterarguments here. For
financial-industrial groups you need if not a common
system then at least a highly homogeneous system of
taxation, legislation, and property relations. Things that
would be easy to do in the framework of a single union
state are not easy to do when you have a cluster of
different states, parliaments, and presidents. When the
USSR disintegrated the common currency and common
credit-finance and insurance systems disappeared too.
Consequently there must be painstaking work to coordi-
nate reform programs in the CIS countries, standardize
laws, and create mechanisms typical of a single state.

Furthermore, complex constructions can easily disin-
tegrate if there are unpredictable changes of political
course in two or three CIS countries. In addition, the
Russian Federation itself does not yet have a full nor-
mative base for financial-industrial groups.

INTERREGIONAL AFFAIRS 21

As a result the agreements on the creation of such
groups with Kazakhstan, Uzbekistan, Belarus, and
Ukraine are marking time. Work with Kyrgyzstan is
also in jeopardy. Projects for financial-industrial groups
which have already been founded remain on paper.
This applies, for example, to the "“Nizhegorodskiye
avtomobili" financial-industrial group involving five
CIS countries and Latvia. Or the "Sokol" financial-
industrial group which is unable to attract colleagues
from Kazakhstan and Kyrgyzstan. The “Interros,"
"Skorostnoy flot," and "Eksokhim" groups are unable
to attract partners from the CIS at the momeni even
though they want to.

From the Files: The "Gazprom" Russian Joint-Stock
Company has joined with Donbass enterprises to form
the "Stal-Truby-Gaz" concern.

The "RUNO" transnational Ukrainian-Russian oil cor-
poration has been founded.

The formation of the "Rogunskaya GES" Joint-Stock
Company is being planned with Tajikistan.

Thus the existing opportunities are currently being
hampered by legal factors and political fragmentation.
At the same time the financial-industrial group is
virtually the only way to save certain sectors of our
industry. And not just a textile industry which has been
left without cotton.

This applies to an even greater extent to the nuclear in-
dustry. After all, six of the [USSR’s] 15 nuclear electric
power stations are now outside the Russian Federation,
along with 75 percent of the commercially viable ura-
nium stocks, monopoly holders in the extraction and
processing of zirconium, beryllium, and rare-earth met-
als, and the experimental base for completing the devel-
opment of nuclear engines.

The Problem With Credits...

Credits are another powerful lever for consolidating and
expanding the Russian economy’s positions within the
CIS. Without credits we could lose sales markets for
our machine building industry.

You do not have to look far for examples. Georgia and
many other "new sovereign states", for instance, would
like to acquire trolleybuses produced by the Uritskiy
plant. (Let us note that Greece and Colombia are happy
to buy them.) But our neighbors do not have the means
to pay — they have no money.

Thus it is necessary to issue credits. Not ordinary credits
but tied credits. In this way they do not leave Russia but
go directly to the supplier enterprise instead. If we do
not give them these credits then Germany, the United

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

22 INTERREGIONAL AFFAIRS

States, Turkey, and Japan will. But that will be for their
output. And we will be forced out of our traditional
markets for decades to come.

But who in Russia is going to give them these credits?
The Ministry for Cooperation with Member-States of
the CIS, the State Committee for the Defense Sectors
of Industry, and the directors of the Mytishchinskiy
machine building plant and the trolleybus plant in
Engels point to the 1996 budget and complain of the
meager figure envisaged there — R200 billion. But Vice
Premier A. Chubays counters that by saying: Tell me
what to cut in order to increase this item of expenditure.

Premier V. Chernomyrdin’s proposal is this: Enlist the
commercial banks and the business sector in coopera-
tion. That makes sense. Indeed, the bankers could give
Georgia the credit that it needs for trolleybuses by fi-
nancing production and in turn could demand a surety
from Tbilisi in the form of land, a sanatorium, or gold
reserves (if it has them).

But there are many obstacles here. For example, our
banks are working to achieve high interest rates at the
moment (as their depositors demand). But the West can
offer Georgia loans at far lower rates.

Second, in the new states the idea of surrendering lo-
cal property to settle debts to Russia is arousing fierce
opposition from the radical nationalists ("Russian im-
perialism!"), which means that this option is extremely
risky for Russian lenders in view of the political insta-
bility in the CIS countries.

Furthermore, the kind of property that could be used
as a surety is often a key asset for the new sovereign
States and a source of foreign currency revenue for their
governments. This can only increase the risk of losing
newly acquired property. Even the accords already
reached with Moldova on the settlement of debts using
property have now been placed in jeopardy.

Third, the acquisition of attractive facilities in the CIS
in settlement of debts will entail investments in those
facilities. However, Russia itself does not have enough
investment funds of its own, the potential of Russian
enterprises is limited, and a considerable proportion
of the potential investment money is going into state
securities.

Thus the huge number of questions created by the
disintegration of the USSR remains unresolved. It is an
extremely important task for Russia to hold on to the
CIS markets, but at the moment they are continuing to
fall into our world competitors’ hands. It is impossible
to repair in an instant something which was smashed
in an instant, so this will require long-term, painstaking
work.

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

First of all the mechanisms of interstate integration must
be bedded in. The rapprochement with Belarus offers us
a unique opportunity. It is probably only then, relying
on well-tried cooperation techniques, that we will be
able to build on success. And even then only after
the meticulous elaboration of integration programs with
each of the CIS countries.

Air Defense System Holds First Joint Exercise

LD1010154395 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
1526 GMT 10 Oct 95

[By Mikhail Shevtsov]

(FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, October 10 (Itar-
Tass) — The CIS integrated air defence system has first
held a multi-national command and staff exercise.

The exercise was conducted under command of Russian
air defence troop commander, air force Colonel-General
Viktor Prudnikov, who is also chairman of the air
defence coordinating committee under the CIS Council
of Defence Ministers.

The exercise was Carried out under a programme of joint
operational exercises of the Russian and CIS air defence
forces.

Prudnikov told Itar-Tass that the CIS integrated air
defence system had improved the level of training of
its personnel and command.

The exercise involved operational forces and comman-
ders of CIS air defence troops.

The exercise was a drill of interactive hunt of border-
trespassing aircraft, anti-hijacking operations and rescue
of distressed and disoriented aircraft.

Control centres of the CIS air defence t.oops and some
30 target- acquisition and fighter planes were used in
the exercise.

General command was carried out from a control centre
of Russian air defence troops.

The CIS integrated air defence system was set up under
an agreement which was signed by CIS heads of state
at the Almaty summit on February 10, 1995.
Moldavia and Azerbaijan stay out of the agreement.

Signatories to the agreement have contributed funds and
manpower to the integrated system.

[By Igor Ivantsov]

(FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW October 10 (ITAR-
TASS) — Talks on the terms, amounts and payments
for Russian natural gas deliveries to Sarajevo began in
Moscow Tuesday [10 October] between the Gazexport
foreign trade company and representatives of Bosnia
and Serbia.

On Monday evening the representatives signed an
agreement with the Gazprom concern regarding the
transit of the Russian gas via their respective territories
to Sarajevo. On Monday night Russia began to pump
the fuel into the pipeline going to the Bosnian capital.

will focus, first and foremost, on the time and form of
payments for the shipments”, he said.

The debt for the Russian fuel delivered to Sarajevo has
amounted to 100 million US dollars since 1991.

Russians, French Dispute Responsibility in Sarajevo

LD1110130895 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
1241 GMT 11 Oct 95

[By Andrey Naryshkin]

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] ZAGREB, October 11 (ITAR-
TASS) — A group of Russian officers at the headquar-
ters of the U.N. force in the Sarajevo sector are con-
cerned over the situation developing in the responsibil-
ity zone of the Russian peace-keeping battalion.

It is the only subunit of “blue helmets” stationed in
"Serb Sarajevo", which controls, from the Serb side,
the confrontation line dividing the capital. All other
battalions (Egyptian, Ukrainian and French) divide
responsibility for the Moslem sectors of the zone.

Such a division suited all when it was "hot" in the
Bosnian capital, and nobody tried to claim rights on
the Russian sector. The situation is now changing,
which was told to ITAR-TASS with concern by Russian
officers.

In their opinion, the matter in question is an attempt
by Frenchmen who occupy key positions at the sector’s
headquarters, to oust Russians from some areas of the
responsibility zone and "to shoulder” some functions
which are under the competer > of Rusbat-2", ranging
from escorting humanitariai;, convoys to Gorazde to
questions of water supplies.

RUSSIAN INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS 25

Tension between the battalions are rising, since the
French, without asking anybody, started patrolling in-
dependently some routes in the Russian sector and hold
separate meetings with local Serb authorities.

To justify their actions, they use a far-fetched pretext
that Russians are short of technical means to cover the
sector completely.

According to some information, they have remapped
zones of responsibility, slashing considerably the Rus-
sian share.

The above prompts an idea that decisions are being
hatched at the end of the operation being carried out
under the U.N. flag, decisions which are aimed at
ousting Russia from a new stage of the peace-keeping
mission where the key role will be given to contingents
from NATO countries.

Moscow Urged To Veto NATO’s Bosnia Plan

MK1110142095 Moscow NEZAVISIMAYA GAZETA
in Russian 11] Oct 95 p2

[Report by Semen Vasilyevskiy under the "Carte
Blanche" rubric: "The Last Nail. Russia Can Itself
Hammer It Into the Coffin of Its Interests in the
Balkans"]

(FBIS Translated Text] The Americans, who have long
been choosing between the military or negotiated way of
imposing their solution on Bosnia, appear to have opted
for the latter. This choice was influenced by fears lest
the conflict spill over into neighboring countries; by the
pent-up displeasure of the West Europeans, who have
been pushed aside from the Bosnian settlement; by an
unwillingness to risk a further heightening of tensions
in relations with Russia; and, most importantly, by the
Bosnian Serbs’ ability to stabilize the front line after
massed NATO air strikes.

In the current circumstances, when the Americans have
all the trump cards in their hand, a decision appears
to have been made provisionally to put purely forcible
methods on the back burner, while not forswearing them
altogether; for what is effectively left to be resolved
are just the two interrelated problems of ensuring a
constitutional arrangement in Bosnia-Herzegovina that
would be suitable for the United States, and legalizing
NATO’s long-term presence in this former Yugoslav
republic. If we do not delve into nuances, the expansion
of the North Atlantic Alliance onto the Balkan region
has been precisely the objective of U.S. policy in
southeastern Europe at least since 1993.

Active steps have already been taken in the two afore-
mentioned areas. At meetings in Geneva, New York,
and Rome, representatives of Croatia, Yugoslavia, and
Bosnia-Herzegovina — in fact, only its Muslim part
— sponsored by U.S. Assistant Secretary of State
Richard Holbrooke, effectively discarded the principle
of equal rights for the two opposing Bosnian sides —
the Muslim-Croatian federation and the Serb Republic.
Instead of the initially expected union of the two enti-
ties, what is at issue at the negotiations now is a state
made up of three — Muslim, Croatian, and Serb —

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

parts. Instead of 50 percent of the rights the Serbs will
be given just one-third. One should not be deluded by
the fact that all decisi »ns are to be made by consensus.
The prewar arrangement of Bosnia-Herzegovina was ab-
solutely the same, which did not prevent the world com-
munity, disregarding the results of a Serb referendum,
from recognizing the republic as an independent state
and declaring the Serbs barbarians and aggressors.

The stance of Russian diplomacy is still full of surprises.
It would seem that it should have insisted at least on two
points. The first point is the right of the Serb Republic
to a confederation with Yugoslavia. This springs from
the principle of the equal rights of the warring parties
in Bosnia, which has repeatedly been upheld by the
world community, particularly, the right to establish
parallel relations with neighboring countries. It would
be pertinent to recall that 18 months ago, under an
agreement signed in Washington, the Muslim-Croat
federation was already given the right to a confederative
association with Croatia. The second point is that in
two or three years’ time after signing the agreement
the Bosnian Serbs must be given the right to hold a
referendum on their attitude to the newly-created union.
This matter has also been widely discussed at various
meetings and sessions. In particular, this is what Jimmy
Carter promised the Bosnian Serbs. But, as the saying
goes, things are still right where they started.

Unfortunately, not only is the Russian Foreign Min-
istry absolutely passive and devoid of backbone, but,
to all appearances, despite all presidential statements, it
is Continuing its line toward isolating the Bosnian Serbs.
Although representatives of the Foreign Ministry have
recently resumed meetings with them after a long pause,
their real actions tell a different story. Take, for instance,
the recent vote by Sergey Lavrov, Russian representa-
tive to the UN Security Council, in favor of sanctions
against the Serb Republic. It’s beyond all understand-
ing how one can extend sanctions with one hand —
and provide humanitarian aid with another! Nor can one
understand the timing of opening a Russian diplomatic
mission in Sarajevo to the Muslim-led government of
Bosnia-Herzegovina, something Haris Silajdzic, premier
of this government, who visited Moscow the other day,
was swift to announce.

Preparations for a NATO operation are also in full
swing. The council of this alliance has already drawn
up its key parameters, the map of carving up Bosnia
into spheres of influence of the United States, Britain,
France, and so forth. About 50,000 servicemen (half
of them Americans) are expected to be subordinate
to a single NATO command and to have “a flexible
mandate,” that is, the right not only to self-defense, but
also to the suppression of those who may defy their

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

orders. Obviously, the issue is the Bosnian Serbs alone.
UN Secretary General Butrus-Butrus Ghali, citing a lack
of funds, also said that a replacement of UNPROFOR
by NATO forces would be desirable. The new operation
is expected to be financed from a specially created
international fund money for which will be given
primarily by Muslim countries.

In order to secure passage of the requisite resolution
through the UN Security Council, the token participa-
tion of Russian forces, first of all in the area of Sarajevo,
is provided for. After the "peacekeeping" — or, effec-
tively, occupation — NATO forces are brought in, the
demilitarization of Bosnia (read, the Serbs) will prob-
ably be announced, and it will be as good as done.
NATO’s de facto presence will also be formalized de
jure.

Judging by the latest statements of Andrey Kozyrev
and other high-ranking Russian diplomats, there is
a realistic possibility that the old, familiar scenario
may recur: Initially certain doubts and concerns will
be expressed, then — since the West will allegedly
give us the requisite assurances and take our demands
into account — the UN Security Council will once
again give the "green" or "yellow" light to NATO’s
expansion. Metaphorically speaking, after adopting anti-
Serb sanctions, allowing NATO aviation to fly over
Bosnia, and authorizing the deployment of ground rapid-
reaction forces there, we will thus with our own hands
hammer the last nail into the lid on the coffin of
Russian interests in the Balkans. Afterward we will be
effectively unable to have any influence in the region.

The options announced by Pavel Grachev — a mod-
ern NATO-Russia operation on a parity basis, with
both parties alternating in command; the creation of
multinational forces from among the troops of NATO,
other countries, and Russia, provided decisions are made
unanimously at all levels — are unfeasible. Not only be-
cause NATO servicemen will never forswear the unity
of command or, moreover, agree to operate under pro-
visional Russian command, but also because, as experi-
ence has shown, presently the Russian Foreign Ministry
is unable to secure fully equal conditions in carrying
out such an operation. It will once again be "taken in,”
all the more so because, to ali appearances, it "is itself
glad to be deceived."

Matters of financing have also been factored out.
Grachev’s scenarios, however, will be a good bargaining
chip if the Americans forgo the continuation of a purely
UN operation, and will also give a pretext finally to use
the right of veto should they be rejected.

It seems that only after the use of a veto will they
start talking with us in earnest and searching for a

RUSSIAN INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS 27

compromise. Only through a veto can we seize our last
chance to remain in the Balkans. By exercising the right
of veto we will also make it clear to the Serbs — both
in Yugoslavia and in the Serb Republic — that they can
count on us and resolutely oppose the NATO presence.
This would preempt NATO countries’ possible attempt
to come to Bosnia "at the invitation" of the warring
factions, not according to a UN mandate.

The hypothetical option to which both NATO and
Russia can agree after a series of consultations is as
follows: completely separate — that is, each under
its own command — NATO and Russian contingents
on the territories of the Muslim-Croatian Federation
and the Serb Republic respectively. Therefore, NATO
countries will be mostly on one side of the international
demarcation line, while the Russian contingent will
be on the other. The UN must continue exercising
overall control over the entire operation and financing
it. Money to finance the operation may be contributed
by any organizations or specially created funds, but
the money must, again, be distributed through the UN.
The figure of 50,000 servicemen requested by NATO
in order to carry out the operation should be slashed
considerably, because in peacetime the main functions
of the multinational forces will boil down to control and
oversight. At the same time, in order to maintain relative
parity, our contingent could be increased somewhat.

Typically, a few days ago, when asked a specific ques-
tion about his attitude to the just described hypothetical
scenario of "Russia and NATO" as opposed to "Russia
within NATO," Nicholas Burns, official spokesman for
the U.S. State Department, made it clear that he does
not rule out such an option.

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

statement delivered to a news briefing in Moscow by

Sarajevo, but that is a technical matter," the minister

"Although specialists from Sarajevo arrived in Moscow
only by evening on October 9, the Russian side suc-
ceeded in persuading them to begin negotiations the

in the morning on Tuesday (Russian gas monopoly)
Gazprom and the Bosnians signed an agreement on tech-
nical questions relating to the resumption of the delivery
of gas. Immediately after that a directive was given to
open the valve in Hungary."

This means Russian President Boris Yeltsin’s initiative
for an immediate cease-fire is going into practice,
Kozyrev said.

"It is the task of all Contact Group members to ensure
that by October 10 the parties to the conflict put into
effect the truce agreement, which must open the way to
the political settlement of the conflict and grow into a
definitive end to the war. No one must be allowed to
block this historic opportunity," said Kozyrev, currently
on a routine visit to Finland.

During the night of October 9 Russia resumed gas sup-
plies to Bosnia, a high-ranking Russian diplomat told
INTERFAX Tuesday. He said this became possible fol-
lowing the solution of a number of technical problems.

On Monday evening, shortly after arriving in Moscow,
a delegation of Bosnian gas-industry experts held talks
with the managers of Russia’s joint-stock company
Gazprom, as a result of which the pipeline leading to
Sarajevo was opened, he said.

According to his sources, Russian and Bosnian experts
reached agreements on the volumes of gas supplies and
on the terms of payment. "The Bosnian side agreed to
partially pay for the gas," said the diplomat.

He said he did not have information on whether the gas
had reached Sarajevo. "Even if it has not, it will in a
couple of hours. Pressure must be raised in the pipeline
and corresponding services are busy doing this," he said.

He pointed out that Gazprom is not a state-run organiza-
tion. "Only previously did the government issue orders
which were instantly fulfilled," he said. He also said that
this particular circumstance explains why the resump-
tion of gas supplies to Bosnia was delayed.

He said the connection between the resumption of
Russian gas supplies and cease-fire in Bosnia was
absolutely incorrect.

Washington Talks on Third World Debts Reviewed

MM1110095695 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian
11 Oct 95 pl

[Report by Irina Savvateyeva: “Russia Hopes To Re-
cover Hopeless Debts"}

(FBIS Translated Text] Washington — At the meetings
Russian Finance Minister Vladimir Panskov has had
within the framework of the latest IMF and IBRD
session some progress has been made on the question
of Russia’s recovering developing countries’ debts.

The total debt to the former USSR exceeds $160 billion.
No progress has been made for some years on the
question of the return of these sums. The stumbling
block is the policy to be followed when calculating the
amount of the debt.

Until recently the sides were reluctant to give ground
on this question. The debtor-countries insisted that the
debt should be calculated on the basis of the current
ruble rate. Russia, for its part, regards the USSR State

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

3» RUSSIAN INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

Bank rate, at around 60 kopecks per dollar, as the only
one that can be used.

But within the framework of recent meetings with the
Nicaraguan foreign economic relations minister and the
Algerian finance minister the foundation has been laid
for an agreement in principle on the possible repayment
of debts in accordance with the following plan. Algeria’s
and Nicaragua’s debts will be calculated in accordance
with the State Bank rate, but Russia will agree to provide
these countries with favorable enough conditions for
repaying the debts, in view of the fact that these debts
are practically hopeless.

At the same time there was a discussion with Nicaragua
of the possibility of issuing its debts as bills, which
subsequently would be able to circulate freely on the
secondary market.

Viadimir Panskov believes that the agreement with
Nicaragua could actually be signed before the end of
this year. The value of the agreements that has been
reached, according to the finance minister, is that they
could establish a precedent for other debtor-countries.

FAX) — Russia’s Foreign Ministry will consider alter-

commercial jet and its crew if negotiations continue to
prove fruitless, a top ministry official told INTERFAX
Tuesday.

The plane and crew of the Russian airline Aerostan have
been held by the Afghan opposition movement Taleban
for two months.

"If [negotiations] prove inadequate, then other ways
will have to be considered," the ministry official said,
However, he preferred not to answer when asked
whether military action was implied.

He made it clear that another round of talks held
on Monday in the Taleban-controlled Afghan city of
Kandahar between Russian and Taleban representatives
gave no hope for an early release of the Russian pilots,
who have been held by the Afghan opposition for over
two months.

"So far we have seen utter intransigence and a large
degree of malevolence on the part of Taleban, and that
shows that they are not prepared for purely humanitarian
solutions,” the source stressed.

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

When Islamic Conference and UN officials had met
on October 9, they demanded freedom for two pilots
whose health aroused fear. "But Taleban answered with
refusal," the diplomat pointed out.

He reported that “one of the two pilots, whose condition
aroused particular anxiety, has been discharged from
hospital."

"Although conditions in local clinics leave much to be
desired, doctors have helped the pilot, and his life is
now out of danger," the source stressed.

He also reported that Taleban handed to the Russian
side in Kandagar on Monday lists of about 6800 Afghan
citizens, whose return is made by the opposition the
major condition for the crew release.

According to the diplomat, Moscow expects to get these
lists by Wednesday.

The source underlined the fact that "the protocol, which
was drawn up during the lists transfer and signed by
UN and Islamic Conference officials, refers to deported
Afghans rather than to those listed as missing.”

"Thus, Taleban drops its former claims," the diplomat
said. Earlier, Taleban alleged that their compatriots had
been moved into former USSR by force.

As the diplomat stressed, the Russian side will try to
locate these persons by using all information at its
disposal.

Daily Says Perry-Grachev Talks Clear Way for
Summit

MMI1010155995 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian
11] Oct 95 p3

[Report by Vladimir Nadein: "Grachev and Perry Clear
Way for Penultimate Meeting Between Yeltsin and
Clinton")

{[FBIS Translated Text] Washington — This year’s
fourth meeting between the Russian and U.S. defense
ministers took place in Geneva on the orders of the two
presidents. Both Clinton and Yeltsin are very concerned
at the state of bilateral relations. Of course, they cannot
be described as poor. There is not the old enmity, but
there is not the new friendship either. Relations are
mediocre. And the most unpleasant thing is that both
Capitals simply cannot shake off the feeling that one day
everything may suddenly become much worse because
of some trifle.

Breaking the unpleasant trend by an entirely strong-
willed step, Boris Yeltsin sent Bill Clinton several
particularly cordial greetings messages. With a joy
that some experts would deem excessive, the Russian

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

congratulated his "friend Bill" on outstanding successes
in both the Near East, where Russian cochairmanship is
seen chiefly from the historical viewpoint, and Bosnia.
Furthermore, Boris Yeltsin pretended he was in no
way annoyed with the three Yugoslav presidents, who
preferred the cozy Washington area to the cozy Moscow
area as a venue for their talks.

Even the White House likes a kind word. William Perry,
hot from chairing the annual meeting of NATO foreign
[as published] ministers, was ordered to fly urgently
to Geneva. There he was awaited by the benevolently
disposed Pavel Grachev.

This time the ministers agreed on a great deal, but
probably the most important thing was the clear idea
of the partners’ positions. The zones of agreement are
clearly designated and the disagreements clearly visible
after Sunday in Geneva.

The United States warmly welcomes participation by
Russian troops in Bosnian peacekeeping. It is not a
question of a symbolic contingent, of some auxiliary
railroad or hospital and humanitarian role. The Western
countries, which have just consulted at defense-minister
level, are prepared to offer Russia something like Boris
Yeltsin's old surprise offering to the local soviets: Take
as much sovereignty as you can handle.

NATO is not restricting the Russians. Send as many
troops for peacekeeping as you can maintain. The
NATO ministers noted that "full Russian partnership in
the Bosnian operation is vitally important to regional
stability.” In addition to the existing two battalions,
President Yeltsin is offering a reinforced division for
peacekeeping duties in Bosnia. This will bring the total
number of Russian soldiers in Yugoslavia to 20,000.
The United States will move exactly the same number
of servicemen into Bosnia, provided, of course, the
administration manages to win the tug-of-war with the
Republican Congress. In all, the NATO countries are
proposing to assign forces of 60,000 men for this
operation.

That is about as far as the zone of agreement between
the two ministers seems to go. The Americans are
sternly insisting that NATO retain operational control.
The Americans feel a strong aversion for the two-year
“dual key" experiment whereby any action in Bosnia
requires the “okay” of both UN representatives and
NATO headquarters officers.

"I do not want to understate the spheres in which
we were unable to reach agreement, where the main
difficulty hinges on how operational control is to be
implemented,” William Perry said after the meeting.
“This is also a question of political control. Our view

RUSSIAN INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS 31

assumes that political leadership of the operation should
come from the North Atlantic alliance, and it is quite
clear that the Russians disagree with this."

Emphasizing that Russia wants to see the international
forces under a UN Security Council mandate, Pavel
Grachev gave the following logical justification of this
position: This will enable other countries that are not
NATO members to detail troops for the noble purposes
of peacekeeping. "We agreed that this must not be called
a NATO operation," Pavel Grachev said. "But the name
must reflect reality.”

The Russian minister’s position would have looked even
more logical if he had been able to offer not only troops
but the funds to maintain them. Here NATO holds all
the trump cards. The UN treasury is empty, and this
venerable organization is greeting its 50th anniversary
on the verge of bankruptcy. Russia has no money.

At this point we should add: Luckily for NATO.
Although the Cold War is long gone, Europeans fear
the likelihood of a repetition of the former occupation
zones. The Russian concept of democracy still does not
match the Western concept. It may be better, but at any
rate it is not the same. The West has different ideas
about when to withdraw, how to withdraw, and what
to take with you. Serious debates could have arisen
in European parliaments if the heroes of the Chechen
war had themselves decided how peace and tranquillity
should best be brought to war-torn Bosnia.

However, this assumption is a long way from reality.
The Americans will be prepared to make several further
minor concessions to Moscow, but they will not counte-
nance sharing influence with Russian generals. And this
Creates problems at the presidential level, in all proba-
bility. It is tempting for Boris Yeltsin to share the laurels
of peace in Bosnia with the West. But poverty leaves
him no choice. Because Russia’s national interests are
connected with its participation in the coming year’s
main international program — the Yugoslav settlement.
Even if effective leadership remains with NATO.

The alternative is isolation in Europe, a missed oppor-
tunity to improve relations with the United States, and
a slow drift away from the other CIS armies, which are
showing increasing interest in the NATO "Partnership
for Peace" program.

Pavel Grachev has helped his president to take the next
step. After Geneva observers’ attention has been riveted
on the clear disagreements between the ministers. And
the very important new agreements have been left in the
shadows, so to speak.

First, a group of Russian generals is immediately
going to Brussels. Officially in order to work on

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

32 RUSSIAN INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

the problems of Bosnia. But that is not all. They
will give specific expression to what Moscow calls
the "special relationship with NATO." And despite
everything Russians will fly to Fort Riley, Kansas, to
take part in the first joint exercises.

And although Pavel Grachev again described as “un-
acceptable" the plans to enlarge NATO via the former
Warsaw Pact members, in the spirit of the new concilia-
tory approach he added that this disagreement must not
affect the mutual desire to seek closer military cooper-
ation with the West.

The joint maneuvers in Kansas begin 23 October, the
precise day that the next summit talks between B.
Yeltsin and B. Clinton will be held in Hyde Park, New
York. These will clearly be the penultimate talks. One
more meeting in Moscow is likely in May of next year,
then both presidents will have other things on their
minds — elections!

LD1010141295 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1312 GMT 10 Oct 95

(FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, Oct 10 (INTER-
FAX) — The Russian president’s national security ad-
viser Yuriy Baturin believes that developments in the
Balkans threaten Russia’s national interests.

"Russia has come too close to the limit after which it
will even lose hope in maintaining its interests in the
Balkans," Baturin told INTERFAX.

the external threat to Russia’s national interests.

maintain equilibrium no return should be made to the
relations of confrontation.

Eastern Europe is our main task in this case. NATO
is taking steps in this direction, Russia is not. This
situation should be improved,” said Baturin.
