UKRAINE 67

Marchuk on Government Program 1995-96

WS1110090095 Kiev URYADOVYY KURYER
in Ukrainian 28 Sep 95 pp 3, 4

[Interview with Prime Minister Yevhen Marchuk by
unidentified correspondent; place and date not given:
“Organizational and Administrative Support for the
Program of Government Activity"]

{[FBIS Transiated Text] [Correspondent] Yevhen
Kyrylovych, the Ukrainian Cabinet of Ministers has
submitted a program of activity to the Supreme Coun-
cil. Does this mean that the government is proposing
something cardinally new to our parliament and society,
something lacking in its former activity?

(Marchuk] I want to emphasize at the very beginning
of our interview that the government perceives its activ-
ity as the process of solidifying the basic provisions of
economic policy announced in October 1994 and devel-
oped in successive speeches by [President] L. Kuchma.
Therefore, in this respect, the government continues to
implement the policy of economic reforms in our soci-
ety. Life, however, usually introduces its own correc-
tions. Therefore, after analyzing the recent situation in
Ukraine’s economy, we have concluded that it is neces-
Sary to introduce certain corrections in our reforms. This
primarily refers to exerting a purposeful influence by the
State on economic processes and phenomena to system-
atize efforts in reforming economic relations. Hence,
the proposed program of the government's activities in-
cludes a number of new approaches and propositions. |
think that it is not necessary to consider novelty an end
in itself. Possibly, an optimal program should harmo-
niously combine rational achievements of the past with
timely innovations oriented toward the future.

[Correspondent] Former governments were also work-
ing out and publicizing their programs, but none of them
has managed to curb the crisis. In your opinion, what
is the difference between the former programs and that
proposed today by your government? On what is your
optimism based?

{[Marchuk] You know, I am not poing to depreciate
the efforts of those working in the government prior
to us; the former governments’ programs, too, included
many interesting ideas and serious proposals. Another
matter is why they have not been implemented. By
analyzing the experience of my predecessors, | come
to the conclusion that even the best program risks
remaining only a declaration of intentions if its authors
have not provided for how to implement it. Therefore,
while working out our own program, we wanted to
implant a structure of implementation. If you read the
program carefully, you will surely pay attention to a

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners

68 UKRAINE

purely practical addendum to the text, which is called
“The Basic Measures Regarding the Implementation of
the Program of Activities of the Ukrainian Cabinet of
Ministers in 1995-1996." This document testifies to our
readiness — in the event when it is adopted — to
start implementing the program immediately, so to say,
“without gaining momentum.” What is more, I must
admit that this seemingly modest working document is
of no lesser importance for me than the program itself.
I perceive this document as one of the guaranties for
the practical implementation of our program. | will say
even more: If we are not hampered, if we are given a
possibility, the program will definitely be implemented.
Then our society will be able to judge by tangible results
the meaning and distinctive features of our program.

{Correspondent} “The Basic Measures..." addendum,
which you mentioned, is a document of a dozen pages
in all. Do you think that this is enough for listing all
implementation mechanisms of the program?

(Marchuk] As a matter of fact, the issue is not ex-
hausted by “The Basic Measures..." document alone.
I mentioned it simply to cite an example. It includes
only urgent steps to be taken in an 18-month period. If
your readers are interested in more details concerning
this issue, I will briefly elucidate it to them. First, the
mechanism of implementation of the government's pro-
gram has been included in the text of the program. Each
chapter, aside from determining the main objectives in
a given branch or sphere, includes the determination of
resources needed to achieve these objectives. In addi-
tion, the Cabinet of Ministers has prepared a draft res-
olution formulating specific organizational measures to
implement the program in 1995-1996. Jointly with nec-
essary material-technical and financial resources, these
measures in the aggregate present a real set of activities
intended to immediately fulfill the tasks mapped out in
the program. At the same time, this set of activities is
very specific, including the dates of implementation and
the names of executors. Using the well-known saying
from the management world, I can say that we have
done everything to give every problem, which must be
resolved, its first, middle, and last name.

Thus, there are people to organize the implementation
of the program, and there are also those to be held
responsible if the program is not implemented.

A special governmental commission has recently in-
spected the discipline in executing resolutions by min-
istries and departments. I must admit that the inspection
has revealed serious problems. A good many edicts and
directives by the president, resolutions by the govern-
ment, as well as laws and other documents are not timely
considered and implemented. Therefore, I think that the

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

implementation of the program is predetermined by the
introduction of discipline and elementary order in the
administration system, as well as by the creation of an
efficient system of oversight on all levels. This is what
we are now working on.

The role of ministries and departments will be changed.
They will have to transform from supplicants asking for
privileges, [credit] limits, and deferments [of payment]
into responsible organizers of financial stabilization in
their areas, which will be able to create necessary
conditions to increase production and raise its quality.

Local bodies of power, too, should assume upon ihem-
selves the full responsibility for implementing the gov-
ernment program at the local level. In this way, the com-
plex of organizational measures will be implemented on
all levels: the government — ministry — and local bod-
ies of power.

{Correspondent} Yevhen Kyrylovych, is the current
structure of ministries and other central bodies of
executive power in Ukraine rational? Is there any
correspondence between the structure of these bodies
and the structure of the program?

(Marchuk] The current structure of state administration
bodies is not optimal. There are many objective and sub-
jective reasons for this. Although there is no straight and
direct correspondence between the structure of the gov-
ernment and that of the program, we will nevertheless
correlate the current structure of the executive power
with the program to bring the functional structure of
the government as accurately and efficiently as possible
in line with the complex of measures and tasks mapped
out by the program. In short, we will assign each set of
the program tasks to a functional unit which will for-
mally be obliged to fulfill these tasks. This does not
always refer to one individual ministry or department.
In such a case, there will be no contradictions between
the structure, functions, and contents of the administra-
tion’s activities.

[Correspondent] To what extent is the administration
vertical structure capable of ensuring the implementa-
tion of the government’s program? There is a threat that
the program may be “buried” at the local level by bar-
riers put up to its implementation, as happened many
times in the past.

{[Marchuk] Your readers probably know very well
that the formation of a viable administration vertical
structure is one of the most iraportant strategic tasks
set by the Ukrainian president. Of course, there are
problems here. Several years of slackening discipline
and responsibility, of impunity and connivance, have
led to the current situation where some elements of

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

the administration vertical structure — both individual
officials and entire sectors — remain unadapted both
organizationally and psychologically, and, possibly, are
already incapable of any efficient management.

However, for the sake of objectivity, it must be said
that the administration today is forced to work under
an extremely tight timetable. In addition, it should be
admitted that the equipment of workplaces does not al-
ways meet modern standards. A portion of experienced
and skilled specialists has left for the private sector, be-

cause the average salary in the state administration is.

not high these days — approximately 36th place among
other job categories.

The government is aware that the success of socioe-
conomic transformations envisioned in its program de-
pends, first and foremost, on efficient work of the state
executive power bodies of all levels. Therefore, we think
that one of the program’s primary tasks is to staff these
bodies with highly skilled employees and to create an
integral system of work with them. The program de-
votes an entire chapter to the personnel issue. Appropri-
ate normative basis and organizational forms are being
created for work with the personnel in accordance with
modern requirements. There is a draft statute of work
with personnel of central and local executive bodies and
executive committees of local councils; a statute of the
formation of reserves of leaders in state enterprises, in-
stitutions, and organization; and a statute of the “Kadry"
single state computer system. There are also envisioned
other specific steps oriented toward enhancing the pres-
tige of the public service and providing the country with
efficient, mobile, and highly skilled administrators who
could constitute a real backbone of public life.

The strengthening of the administration vertical struc-
ture today is a necessary condition for fulfilling all our
plans and tasks, including the government’s program.
After the signing of the Constitutional Agreement be-
tween the president and the Supreme Council, all nec-
essary legal preconditions have been created. Now it
is the turn of organizational-administrative efforts. The
government will not spare such efforts. The administra-
tion vertical structure will be as it has to be — sturdy,
energetic, and viable. If anybody in the center or at
local level tries, as you put it, to "bury" that part of
the program, which he has to implement according to
his position, he will immediately feel that the vertical
structure does exist and is not “asleep.” I would like to
avoid disciplinary measures; I would like all employees
to prove that their awareness and responsibility meet
the requirements of time. Therefore, only concerted and
intense work of the entire state administration from top
to bottom can ensure the implementation of the govern-
ment’s program.

UKRAINE 69

{Correspondent} With what other resources, aside from
administrative, do you plan to ensure the implementa-
tion of the program?

([Marchuk] The government thinks that working out and
adopting a package of legislative and other normative
acts will be one of the most important tasks in imple-
menting the government’s program of activities. The
program is supplemented by a list of laws, presidential
edicts, and governmental resolutions which either are
being or have already been worked out, and which are
to constitute the legal basis ensuring the implementation
of the program. I will note that the Cabinet of Minis-
ters has already submitted 125 draft laws to the fourth
session of the Supreme Council. They include 16 draft
laws of the social-cultural nature and 13 concerning is-
sues of combating organized crime, a matter of great
concern for people today. There are also many eco-
nomic draft laws, in particular, 12 on macroeconomy,
seven on ownership and entrepreneurship, and six on
the agro-industrial complex and food provisions. There
are also draft laws on environment, the fuel and energy
complex, as well as science and new technologies.

The government's legislative work will be focused on
achieving the main goal — stabilizing the production
sector and heightening the level of people’s life. Le-
gal acts will allow the program’s provisions to become
mandatory for state bodies and public officials. The non-
fulfillment of these provisions in accordance with the
law will entail responsibility — disciplinary, ‘adminis-
trative, and even criminal.

I will also note that we have fixed specific dates of
preparation and specific executors for each of these legal
acts, which and who should ensure the implementation
of our projects.

Thus, the organizational-legal mechanism for imple-
menting the government’s program has been created and
its tasks have been determined. This is a guarantee that
the program's provisions will be implemented.

[Correspondent] As is known, you can arrive at the
same goal going along different roads. What road, out
of many, has the Ukrainian Government chosen? Do
you not think that it may prove wrong or too long?

{[Marchuk] It is a difficult question. There is no doubt
that the government has no euphoric expectations of
quick success. The road leading to economic stabiliza-
tion does not promise to be short. Oriental sages used
to say that every long road starts with a small step. To
make this step, we should choose the main directions
on which the government will concentrate its efforts.
The program determined 11 such directions, ranging
from social to financial policies. Instead of listing all of

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

70 UKRAINE

them, let me emphasize the main thing: Special empha-
sis will be put on concreteness and practical implemen-
tation of all those directions. As an example, let us take
the sphere of industrial reconstruction that is so critical
for reforming the economy. The government’s program
does not simply declare the need for reconstruction, but
it also specified urgent measures aimed at practical im-
plementation of the reconstruction. They include over-
coming non-system structural distortions in restorable
and inter-industrial proportions [as published], stimu-
lating the development of industries with quick capital
turnover and high-technology industries, reconstruction
of unviable enterprises, creation of financial-industrial
groups, etc. I would least of all like to bore our readers
with professional terminology, but we cannot do with-
out the economic categorical apparatus [as published] in
our conversation. I would like people to understand that
the government is not conducting a loose talk about a
plan of its activities; instead, it has determined concrete
economic and management measures to make goals de-
clared in the program a reality. Moreover, every provi-
sion in the government’s program is a reference point
for ministries and institutions, based on which they de-
vise — and some of them have already done so — their
specific industry programs.

Thus, we now have a coherent system of implementing
the government's program, covering the Cabinet of
Ministers leadership, industry ministries, as well as the
actual production sphere and socio-economic processes.

As for correctness of our road, a thorough analysis
and examination of the program by qualified specialists
allows us to hope that we have chosen the correct path.
On the other hand, I remember [German philosopher]
Hegel's saying that practice is the best criterion of the
truth. The practice will show how correct our program
was.

[Correspondent] Until now, all attempts to improve
things at the macroeconomic level have for some reason
worsened them. How will the next attempt to stabilize
the country’s economy affect household economies?
Will the price of Government's forces not prove too
costly for people’s pockets?

{Marchuk] I understand such fears. Unfortunately, peo-
ple have negative experiences connected with the falling
standard of living and skyrocketing prices. Some of
them link the deteriorated life conditions with reforms.
Well, we, indeed, are walking along a difficult road, and
there are quite a few sad things and disappointments
lurking around on it. History shows, however, that all
states went out of crisis for a long time and with com-
plications, and all of them went through a period of
hardships and misery. We are not an exception here,

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

unfortunately. In addition to problems common for all
post-Soviet countries, we have to correct our own mis-
takes. But there is only one way out — pressing ahead.

One of the key chapters of the government’s program
was devoted to social policies. We want to give as
much attention to the social sphere as possible. And
again, these are not just declarations. We came up with
urgent social protection measures, such as a reform
of payments and a system of assistance to financially
insecure social groups, families with children, as well
as partial and full benefits to the unemployed. It is
planned to set up an empioyment service and a system
of social insurance. There are specific plans regarding
implementation of the housing policy.

Of course, the sorest issue is obtaining funding for
social programs. The government is ready to support
any well grounded initiative which could, at least a bit,
improve people’s life. And in this sphere, we do not
display, so to say, the “ministerial egoism.” Here is a
specific example. During a meeting with me, people’s
deputy V. Kolomoytsev proposed that I review the draft
“Law on Indexation of Citizens’ Incomes” that he made.
Despite the Ukrainian president’s edict on indexing
savings by 2,200, I immediately signed a directive
ordering relevant ministries and institutions to make a
coordinated decision on this. If this draft law proves to
be thoroughly made and realistic, the government will
seek to adopt it. And it does not matter that this bill was
not proposed by a deputy, and instead by a government
official. Let me declare it once again, the government is
firmly resolved to cooperate with the parliament and all
political forces to foster social protection of people and
gradual but steady improvement of their life. This has
been said in the program’s social protection part, and it
is a permanent guideline for the government’s activities.

[Correspondent] The program consists of more than 100
pages, and it is full of specialistic terminology, figures,
tables, and charts. It is very easy to get confused by all
this. How would you describe the program’s prime goal?
What is the most important task that the government
wants to accomplish?

(Marchuk] Let me put it in simple terms, we want
Stabilization. Before we press ahead, we must stop our
sliding down. Not only does the unbalanced economy
worsen people’s life, but it also acts as a delayed-action
mine planted in our young statehood. To survive as
an independent state and keep developing, we need
to stabilize all components making up our economy.
Purely financial remedy tools have so far not produced
the expected results. The monetary methods should be
organically complemented with organizational methods
of reforming economic processes.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

In recent years, we were told so much about the
efficiency of economic self-regulation which the market
helps carry out under liberalized approaches that we
came to fear the word "regulation," equating it for
some reason with the centrally administrated economy.
I think that this is wrong. A balanced and prudent
regulation of economic processes which will help to
mend the national economy is not only possible, but
also necessary. This is what many countries do.

Let me emphasize that we are not renouncing market
tools. On the other hand, we understand that in the
present situation, the lever of state regulation is abso-
lutely necessary. It is in the combination of market and
State regulation mechanisms that we see an optimal op-
portunity to achieve our main goal — stabilization of
production and the economy in general.

[Correspondent] The every-day life, and not only sta-
tistics, proves that imported goods are increasingly re-
placing Ukrainian products in the domestic consumer
market. Is it possible that we have lost our ability to
produce what we produced several years ago? What is
the government planning to do to support domestic man-
ufacturers?

{Marchuk] The strategy of the government’s program
is entirely aimed at the development of domestic pro-
duction. It is planned to devise production programs,
actively look for sources of their financing, develop a
system of inter-state projects. Of course, it is necessary
that we activate the entire economic system to boost pro-
duction. Production is the final element of the economic
chain. This is why it is planned to introduce changes in
financial, credit, price, and budgetary policies so that
those spheres stimulate production, instead of restrain-
ing it. It is planned to accumulate funds, also by actively
attracting foreign investments, as well as to hasten struc-
tural changes both at the macroeconomic and microe-
conomic levels, including the state sector.

Also necessary will be familiar, although recently ne-
glected, harsh economic austerity methods to save ma-
terial, first of all energy and financial, resources. It is
planned to strengthen scientific and innovative activities
and modernize production.

A targeted program of entrepreneurship in the techno-
logical sphere will be crafted and antimonopoly and
competition-encouraging measures undertaken.

These and other planned measures are intended to stop
the drop in production and stabilize the situation in the
national economy so that the domestic manufacturer is
a genuine master on the domestic market. Let imported
goods be present in our market, but only as a garnish
rather than the main dish. I hope that all of us will live

UKRAINE 71

to see the times when the competitiveness of Ukrainian
products will let them penetrate all international mar-
kets. Already, the government is trying to lay serious
fruitful groundwork for this long-term and complicated
program, which is its plan of action.

[Correspondent] Ukraine is actively integrating into the
international community, including in the economic
sphere. How can the international situation impact
implementation of the government program?

{(Marchuk] On the international arena, too, Ukraine
seeks the same thing as in its internal affairs — stability.
The government seeks to create and sustain a system of
foreign relations that would secure a worthy place in
the world for Ukraine, guarantee its security, and open
up new opportunities for fruitful economic cooperation.
Ukraine’s economic interests should be fully protected.
For all hardships connected with the transition period,
our country is not a colony but a powerful European
State with huge potential. The government intends to
utilize this potential better than in the past.

Specific measures will make our foreign policy active,
preemptive, stable, and flexible. We should fully restore
the system of economic commonwealth with Russia and
other CIS countries, as well as to speed up integration
with European and world structures, such as the EU,
OSCE [Organization of Security and Cooperation in
Europe], and CEFTA [Central European Foreign Trade
Agreement].

The active foreign policy is starting to bring results.
Ukraine has been admitted to the European Council, its
voice sounds more weighty, and the respect to us on the
part of the world community is growing.

International financial assistance is a separate issue.
Such assistance certainly facilitates resolution of some
of our problems, and we will conduct active negotiations
to attract international financial aid to Ukraine. With all
due respect to such aid, I must say, however, that we
should rely on our own forces in the first place. We may
receive assistance, but no one will maintain us. This
is why the government will rely primarily on its own
efforts and coordinated and busy cooperation of all the
people for the sake of our children and grandchildren.

[Correspondent] Do you think that the government's
program may become a card in political games inside
the country? After all, everyone knows that various
political forces have mixed opinions about it.

{Marchuk] There are two forecasts regarding debates
on the government's plan in the Supreme Council. A
pessimistic forecast says that the plan will be voted
down under all circumstances, while the optimistic one
says that all fears are in vain, because the program will

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

72 UKRAINE

be passed without particular problems. I believe that the
truth is somewhere in the middle. Legislative debates
promise to be heated, but the program stands a realistic
chance for passage.

I personally divide opinions about this plan into two
categories. If it is a qualified and serious analysis, and
if the author of this opinion really wants to improve the
program and cares for the economy and state, I would
like then to see all such proposals carefully examined
and, if possible, taken into consideration.

I stand for active dialog and cooperation with all
political forces. And it was at my initiative that I met
on 22 September with leaders of the Supreme Council
faction and deputy groups. Although our views did not
always agree, I realized that we can come to terms with
each other and that a constructive cooperation based on
healthy compromise is possible. What is needed is the
political will displayed by both sides. I declare with
all responsibility: The Cabinet of Ministers does have
the will to interact and cooperate. I hope that the other

FBIS-SOV-95-196

side also has it. If so, debates on this program could be
held in a balanced, responsible, and constructive manner
in parliament. Everyone, both the state and the people,
will benefit from this. Enough of arguments — we must
work. The government is all set for this.

Hryb Interviewed on Russia’s Integration
Referendum

WS1110093495 Minsk SVABODA in Belarusian
6 Oct 95 pl

{Interview with Belarusian Supreme Soviet Chairman
Myacheslaw Hryb, by Aleh Hruzdzilovich; place and
date not given: "I Have Not Supported This, And I Am
Not Going To Support It"}

[FBIS Translated Text] Responding to the Russian
Federation Council’s decision to hold a referendum
on the unification of Russia and Belarus, Belaru-
sian Supreme Soviet Chairman Myacheslaw Hryb
answered the following questions.

{Hruzdzilovich] Mr. Hryb, how would you comment on
the Russian Federation Council's statement on holding
a referendum on deepening economic and political
integration between Russia and Belarus?

{Hryb] Every country has the right to hold referendums
on issues which are not in variance with that coun-
try’s law and in which people are interested. However,
when a referendum was held in Belarus questions were
asked in a different plane than that proposed by Rus-
sia’s Federation Council. We asked: "Do you support
the strengthening of economic integration with Rus-
sia,” and our people — including myself — answered
"Yes." However, currently Russia’s Federation Coun-
cil proposes a more expanded question: "Do you sup-
port economic and political integration?” It is clear what
economic integration means; however, what does po-

WESTERN REGION 73

litical integration mean? Where is the beginning of it,
and where does it end? It could be understood in vari-
ous ways, including te unification of two countries into
one. However, the question put in such an expanded way
contradicts our Constitution, which says that the Repub-
lic of Belarus is a unitary, sovereign, and independent
State. Everything that runs counter to the Constitution
is explicitly rejected by me. I have not supported this,
and I am not going to support it.

I would like to point another aspect. In my opinion,
the way our neighbors act is not quite correct, for
they propose to hold a referendum on issues which run
counter to the constitution of a neighboring state.

{Hruzdzilovich] Is it conceivable that this is the first
sign of playing the Belarusian card on the eve of the
elections in Russia?

{Hryb] I cannot rule this out. Indeed, public attention is
being drawn to this issue, including proposals to hold the
referendum on the day of elections. We, too, held our
referendum on the day of elections and I am beginning
to realize that we made a mistake. The elections suffered
from that, since all attention was paid to the referendum.
As a matter of fact, observers from international groups,
including from the Council of Europe, have arrived at
the same conclusion.

(Hruzdzilovich] How do you assess the Belarusian pres-
ident’s role in the appearance of the Russian Federation
Council statement? Do you not think that this is the kind
of response to his initiatives expressed during the recent
visits to Moscow and Sochi?

{Hryb] It is very difficult for me to assess that, since the
president's trips to Moscow, Sochi, and Kiev had not
been coordinated with me, and I found out about them,
just as others, from the press. After the visits were paid,
I was not given any additional information.

Assembly Discusses Federation With Top Russian
Officials

WS1110102295 Minsk BELAPAN in English
0806 GMT 11 Oct 95

development and reconstruction of the Belarusian oil-

integration within the framework of Slavneft activity.
The source informed BELAPAN thai the parties had
signed a protocol in which the Belarusian party backed
the Russian government’s decision to alter the compo-
sition of the company and to increase its authorized
stock (The Russian Government Resolution No. 867 of
1 September 1995).

According to the protocol, "Slavneft" will resort to
the mediation of the German firm “Lurgi” which
reached an agreement with the Investment Credit Bank
“Deutscheindustriebank" for attracting its credit funds
to the amount of $260-300 million on security of
"Slavneft" to finance the reconstruction of the Mozyr
refinery, a branch establishment of Slavneft. Before
the completion of the reconstruction of the Mozyr
refinery’s catalytic reforming, Slavneft will allocate $5
million to the refinery from its own resources until 1
January 1996 and another $5 million until 1 May next

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

year on condition 50 percent of the Mozyr refinery
shares plus one share are contributed to the authorized
capital of “Slavneft" in exchange for its shares.

larus and "Slavneft" before the aforesaid credit agree-

Qazhygeldin on 3-Year Reforms Program

964F0017A Almaty PANORAMA in Russian
30 Sep 95 No 38, p2

[Askar Aukenov report on press conference given by
Prime Minister Qazhygeldin on 26 September; place
not given: “Prime Minister Qazhygeldin: People Are
Tired of Reforms and Need Tangible Proof of Their
Expediency—In the Head of Government’s Opinion, the
Cost of Measures To Prepare the State Administration
Apparat Move to Akmola Can Be Kept Under $100
Million")

[FBIS Translated Text] On 26 September, Prime Minis-
ter Qazhygeldin held a press conference devoted to the
main directions of the medium-range government pro-
gram of intensified reforms in 1996-98.

Despite the topic of the meeting and the (very long)
press release that was distributed, the invited journalists
remained in almost total ignorance regarding the actual
content of the government's new program. Therefore
their questions mainly revolved around operational
points and the current economic situation in the country.

To the question as to whether the increase in expendi-
tures on social needs (which is built into the new gov-
ernment program and which the president has been in-
sistently demanding lately) would cause another surge
of inflation, Mr. Qazhygeldin remarked that the head of
state is obliged to fulfill the social mandate of his vot-
ers. Besides, the time has passed for such statements as
“wait some more, it will be better tomorrow.” People
have become tired of reforms and need graphic proof
of their expediency—first and foremost with respect to
growth in the population's income. Finally, there is also
economic expediency in such measures—when income
grows continuously in an environment of declining in-
flation, this money may become an additional reserve
for reinvestment in the economy. However, this requires
that one condition be met: not losin, *>e results achieved
in limiting “mad money.”

"I think I am not a person who can be pushed into rush
actions in the area of macroeconomics. We understand
very well the potential consequences of such steps,” the
prime minister assured. He said that the government
intends to concentrate attention on raising the incomes
of state employees, since “it is no longer possible to
demand that they make a full effort and at the same
time not pay their salaries.” Lately this category of
the population has been increasingly often compelled to
take jobs in alternative sectors, and those most actively
leaving are highly skilled economists and lawyers.

CENTRAL ASIA 75

Regarding the situation with the Roskontrakt com-
pany, which had been lobbying for some Kazakhstani
enterprises to be transferred to its management, Mr.
Qazhygeldin recalled that as far back as in 1994 it was
offered the opportunity to take under management sev-
eral warehouse-terminals on the republic territory and
Carry out distribution on the consignment principle. In
addition to aforementioned facilities, the Russian struc-
ture was interested in some large Kazakhstani enter-
prises manufacturing fertilizer. Currently it has been
asked to put together and present the needed capital.
At the same time, the head of the government hinted
that he has doubts about Roskontrakt’s financial capa-
bilities.

On the subject of which stage—decline or rise—the
republic economy currently is in, after all, the prime
minister remarked that the new medium-range program,
much as the previous one, is an anticrisis program by
nature. The presence in it of a host of issues involving
enterprise reform and the financial and banking system
recovery indicates a crisis. This definitely cannot be
called a stage of growth. The goal is to increase
industrial production in some sectors, and through this
to stem the overall industrial decline.

Nevertheless, Mr. Qazhygeldin noted that while over
nine months of 1994 we saw GDP fall by 31 percent,
currently this indicator has shrunk to 26 percent. “If we
manage to achieve 25 percent by the end of this year
and keep up this trend next year, in the second half of
1996 or in 1997 we may actually have growth,” he said.

Regarding arrears in paying pensions, which, according
to information of the journalist who asked this question,
amount to about 2 billion tenge [T], the head of
the government explained that this amount, strictly
speaking, is not the government's debt but mainly the
debt of territorial pension funds. The amount for which
the government is directly liable is less than T800
million. It arose last year because of the lag that formed
when the minimum wage was raised. The prime minister
promised that it will be repaid before 15 October—this
task was set at the latest meeting of the Cabinet of
Ministers. He said that the government actually loaned
T3 billion tenge to pension funds in 1995.

Mr. Qazhygeldin also pointed out that in those oblasts
where industrial production is picking up (and especially
where large enterprises have been placed under the
management of foreign companies), arrears in pensions
have been liquidated. “We would like to see this happen
everywhere,” he emphasized.

Regarding new approaches to forming free economic
zones [SEZ’s] and the expediency of giving such
status to Akmola (for the purpose of speeding up its

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

76 CENTRAL ASIA

development), the prime minister reported that a group
of specialists currently is working on improvements to
the law on SEZ’s. Some local, limited zones will be
created within its framework. On the other hand, in the
opinion of the head of the government, turning the entire
republic or whole cities into SEZ’s in the current general
state of the economy appears unrealistic, unattractive,
and inexpedient.

The idea of turning the port of Aktau into an off-
shore zone was very tempting; however, in this case
major Western countries are unlikely to grant Kaza-
khstan most-favored-nation status in trade and ratify
agreements on eliminating double taxation.

The prime minister added that Almaty in any case will
remain the nation’s second capital, the financial center
of Kazakhstan and Central Asia, and one of the most
respected cities in the CIS: "Even if we board the train
tomorrow and leave, half of the best brains will still stay
here. That is life."

He categorically rejected the possibility of a hidden
outflow of budget money into the nonbudget fund for
moving the capital: "All matters related to the move
to Akmola depend on the amount of proceeds going
into this fund. Therefore, do not get the idea that a
decision has been made and tomorrow everybody will
take off. Definitely, nobody will move before the end
of the year—there is no money."

In the prime minister’s opinion, the cost of measures
that need to be carried out in Akmola before the actual
move takes place can easily be kept under $100 million,
although, in his words, if one wanted to, this amount
could be inflated as high as $100 billion.

To the request for a comment on the persistent rumors
regarding imminent personnel reshuffling and structural
changes in the government, Mr. Qazhygeldin said that
he has never said anything that would give anyone rea-
son to write anything like this in the press, and therefore
advised journalists to ask each other about it. "When I
read this I could only laugh," he continued. "We decided
at least once to disappoint you in your forecasts. Simply
to avoid creating a commotion around this issue. Other-
wise, it may set a precedent—you wrote this, and then
the government has to do something. Although rotation
is a normal phenomenon. You understand, of course,
that when the prime minister selects his team, he is run-
ning some risks: He takes some people on promise, and
some cannot quite handle the job. Therefore, the ques-
tion of 22 September in principle was raised by you.
At the same time, when I presented the draft of my
medium-range program, I asked the president to let me
exercise my constitutional right and submit to him a
proposal on personnel rotation. The president gave me

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

10 days. Which sectors were criticized? In the past, we
were absolutely unsuccessful in implementing reforms
at enterprises. Heads of agencies tasked with this just
did not have the guts. Everybody jumped at the idea of
transferring enterprises to outside management after the
first precedent was set. Meanwhile, it was outlined in
the first program, and nobody paid any attention to it
at the time. So now everybody wants to do everything
this way—transfer enterprises to outside management. I
disagree. We have to shore up enough courage and say
that with the current reappraisal of fixed assets of en-
terprises we intend to privatize, some of them will have
to be given away in order to save production. Their
market value is zero. We have not succeeded in reform-
ing wages; we have not been able to move away from
the minimum wage mechanism, which today is simply
laughable. It worked well at the time of the single ruble
zone, but now we need completely different indicators—
the consumer basket, percentage of price increases, and
so on. We have completely failed in reforming the pen-
sion system. From the legal standpoint, we are only just
approaching health care reform. I could cite some ob-
jective reasons. The law on bankruptcy was delivered
to us too late, and we still do not have a law on insur-
ance. But this does not get us off the hook. Kazakhstani
citizens could not care less what we have or have not
succeeded in—this is not their headache."

With respect to other rumors—that work is currently
underway on creating a huge political electoral bloc
headed by the prime minister (along the lines of Russia
Is Our Home), the latter remarked that the situation
regarding elections in Kazakhstan differs somewhat
from that in Russia. "We simply have no need now for
a ‘Kazakh home,’" the head of the cabinet joked. "I, at
least, have not heard anything to this effect. There will
be regular elections to the National Assembly. And in
principle, when coordination and the legal boundaries
of institutions of authority are clearly defined, I am not
worried at all about who will be elected. Although I
would like very much to see more economists, lawyers,
and reform-minded people—it will make our job easier.”

Regarding negotiations with the Russian prime minis-
ter in Uralsk, where, according to some reports, a final
agreement was reached on the project of building the
oil pipeline through Russia, Mr. Qazhygeldin explained
that in this case the topic under discussion was not
any particular project. The negotiations revolved around
collaboration in the area of oil and gas sales in gen-
eral. In his words, complete understanding was reached
on all strategic issues. In addition, the SP [joint ven-
ture] LUKoil-Kazakhstan is now being set up—Russian
Capital intends to participate in the development of oil
and gas fields on the territory of our republic because

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

Russia’s own resources are being depleted. If we find
enough capital for the KTK [Caspian pipeline construc-
tion consortium], we will build the KTK; if we find
capital for the KTK with some modifications, we will
build it with modifications," the prime minister summed
up. “But we are fully aware that for the next 15-20 years
the Black Sea is not a solution for us. Russia is aware
of this too. Which means we have to think about transit
through Russia, Ukraine, and Europe, through Russia,
the Baltics, and the Baltic Sea, or through the Mediter-
ranean, access t) which is tremendously restricted for
us. The problem is not Russia. There is the organiza-
tion of OPEC, which is much more powerful than the
individual countries. There is a project that envisages
a gateway through China to the countries of Southeast
Asia. Both Russia and Kazakhstan are part of all the
projects. The main issue is capital."

Karimov Hopes For Greater OSCE Role in
Regional Problems

LD1010202295 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1821 GMT 10 Oct 95

(From the "Presidential Bulletin" feature}

(FBIS Transcribed Text] Uzbekistan is prepared to take
an active part in UN and OSCE ecological and political
problems in the region and to finance them, President
Karimov has said. He was speaking as he received
OSCE Secretary-General Wilhelm Hoink in Tashkent
on Monday. (OSCE leader arrived in Tashkent for a
four-day seminar on the restoration of the environment,
being held at the initiative of the OSCE and the Uzbek
State Committee for Nature Conservation.)

Karimov explained that in the first place he had in mind
measures to save the Aral sea and promote stability in
Central Asia.

Uzbekistan and OSCE said they were concerned about
a number of issues, including ways of protecting the
environment, restoring the ecological balance and pro-
moting stabilization in the region at a time when trouble
spots in Afghanistan and Tajikistan remained. The two
sides agreed that those problems negatively affected the
50-million population of Central Asia.

CENTRAL ASIA 77

At the same time Karimov and Hoink agreed that OSCE
could and must take a more active part in handling
ecological and political problems.

deswehr units within the framework of support for mil-
itary training. As you know, some of your officers are
already undergoing instruction at German military col-
leges, but we should like to expand our activity in this
area, because I think that this will bring only benefit and
will strengthen our relations even further.

{Correspondent} Are you satisfied with the results of
your visit and did you manage to do everything that
you had planned?

[Wiltz] Undoubtedly. We had absolute mutual under-
standing in tackling the majority of issues with the staff
of the presidential apparatus, the Ministry of Foreign
Affairs, and the Ministry of Defense. That is why we
managed to solve in such a short period the range of im-
portant tasks that we had planned. However, I believe
that the main result of our visit is not only the signing of
the agreements, but the establishment of good personal
contacts as well. Our future cooperation will depend to
a large extent on this. [passage omitted]

for any possible development.

on the decision taken on the oil pipeline route, and

Over this one year much was said and done about

tor the implementation of the contract since Septem-
ber 1994. Its importance lies in that all the measures
and plans envisaged by the consortium and AIOC are

were many doubters in some countries and circles.

envisioned plan.

Today you have adopted an important decision on the
construction of the pipelines for the export of early oil.
Two pipelines. One pipeline will take the early oil to

are the experts and naturally have taken the problem's
both economic and technical, and all other aspects into

Pakistan’s President Leghari Addresses Parliament

BK1010155595 Islamabad Radio Pakistan Network

{[FBIS Translated Text] President Farooq Ahmad Khan
Leghari has expressed confidence that the international
community, particularly friendly countries like Azerbai-
jan, will exert pressure on India to end the repression in
occupied Kashmir and to grant the Kashmiri their right
of self-determination in accordance with UN Security
Council resolutions. He was addressing the parliament
of the Republic of Azerbaijan in Baku this evening.
He said he hoped the world community would use its
influence to force India to engage in substantive talks
with Pakistan to resolve the Kashmir issue. He said In-
dia must realize that holding fake elections will neither
fool the world, nor restore its tarnished image. President
Leghari said India continues to commit massive human
rights violations against the Kashmiri people. He said
that Indian brutalities in occupied Kashmir have further
alienated the Kashmiri people. He expressed Pakistan's
thanks to Azerbaijan for supporting the cause of the
Kashmiris.

Regarding Afghanistan, the president said that the
continuing instability and conflict in the country has
brought untold miseries to the Afghan people. It is
imperative that we work together to bring peace to
Afghanistan. The president said we must take into
account the interests of all Afghan groups to bring about
a lasting peace there.

Referring to Azerbaijan's conflict with Armenia, Presi-
dent Leghari said that Pakistan condemns Armenian ag-
gression and wants the occupation forces to withdraw
from the territories captured by them.

The president urged the people of Azerbaijan and their
leaders to join the people and leaders of Pakistan in their
efforts to bring about rapid development and resolve
issues of common concern like environmental pollution,
drug abuse and trafficking, and pressure of population
on resources. In this connection, he said, Pakistan is
ready to share its experience with Azerbaijan.

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

Radio Pakistan’s correspondent Anwar Saeed from
Baku said that Azerbaijani Parliament members greeted
President Leghari’s comments with thundering applause
during his speech.

i will give a little bit more consiueration to the economic

of the lari. Last week, our community's attention was
focused on the introduction of national currency. In
compliance with a decree by the head of state, the
lari is being established as the only legal tender in
Georgia. It is probably still too early to draw final
conclusions but, on the whole, the events are developing
adequately. Today, the lari has an absolute dominance
in all official transactions as the only legal tender
and this is an achievement of key significance during
the implementation of the reform. The process of
establishing the lari is advancing mainly as planned and
Stipulated by the program. [passage indistinct]

I believe that the agreement will be signed by the end

It will be a powerfu! incentive for the revival of the
economy of the whole country. As I have already said,
the European Union will allocate 200,000 tonnes of
grain - this is aid which is not supposed to be paid
off: we have to sell it and fill the budget but the EU
gives it to us free of charge - 30-40,000 tonnes of fuel
oil, funds to repair the railway and to bring our seaports

CAUCASUS 83

to proper condition. In addition, the European Bank will

medium-sized business and will set up an investment

with that, the United States have allocated to Georgia
additional 30,000 tonnes of grain and 100,000 tonnes
of fuel oil. Aid provided by Germany is especially
significant. This is over DM20m which will be used
for the restoration of the Tbilisi thermal power station.

[passage omitted] There was a sharp decline in the

covered by commercial banks. You may recall that
some time ago the demand reached $2.6 million. |
mentioned that in my previous interviews. The exchange
rate was 1.3 lari for one dollar. There was no demand
for U.S. dollars at all at the trades on Wednesday
and Friday, while commercial banks supplied $680,000
and $656,000 respectively. Under these circumstances,
in order to prevent sharp fluctuations at the internal
currency market, the National Bank permitted a drop
in the rate of the dollar to the minimum, that is, 1.28
lari against one U.S. dollar. No demand for the Russian
ruble was observed during the past week. It was the
first time ever that this happened. Altogether 680 million
rubles [R] were supplied. And again, in order to prevent
sharp depreciation of the Russian roble and to meet the
interests of the population, the National Bank purchased
the excess of the rouble at the rate of 25 tetri against
R1,000. [passage omitted]

It is very important to note that the currency market is
getting stable in the circumstances when the currency
reserves of the National Bank increase rather than
reduce. I want you to listen attentively what I am going
to say. The purchase of the currency at the internal
market over the past ten days is expected to result in the
increase of the currency reserves of the National Bank
by $20 million. So, forecasts by some sceptics that «
large amount of currency, including credits allocated
by the IMF and the World Bank, will be necessary
to spent in order to maintain tie power of the lari do
not come true. Incidentally, $25 million allocated as the

the presumption on which I based my statement that we

KOMSOMOLSKAYA PRAVDA Dossier

Igor Giorgadze is 45. He was born into a military family.
His father, Panteleymon Giorgadze, was a career general
in the USSR Border Guards. He is currently leader of
the United Communist Party of Georgia and a candidate
for Georgian president.

Igor Giorgadze graduated from the Moscow KGB
Higher School and has the rank of lieutenant general. He
is a lawyer by education and knows foreign languages
(Turkish and French). He has worked in intelligence
and counterintelligence. He has been awarded the title of
Honored KGB Staffer for fulfilling operational missions.

He saw action in Afghanistan. Returning to Georgia,
he worked for a tine as the republic’s deputy secu-
rity minister, bu. soon resigned owing to his disagree-
ment with President Gamsakhurdia’s policy. Switching
to Defense Ministry structures, he set up a counterin-
telligence directorate. In October 1993 he became head
of the Georgian Security Service. He was dismissed in
early October 1995,

Igor Giorgadze is married with two children.

|Gamov] Igor Panteleymorovich, for some reason you
do not much look like the kind of person who is being
sought day and night by the Georgian special services
and now by the Russian militia as well, or someone who
is supposedly being hunted by hired killers. You're not
even in disguise....

{Giorgadze] What are you on about! Why should I do
that? My profession allows me to behave in a way that
will not upset anyone or attract undue attention.

{Gamov] Nonetheless, you are the currently the focus
of a lot of attention. Your picture is constantly in
the newspapers and on TV. You are suspected of
being iavolved in the attempt on the life of Eduard
Shevardnadze and in an attempt to stage a coup d’etat....

|Giorgadze] To be honest, the most hurtful thing is not
the accusation ttself, but the suspicions about my unpro-
fessionalism. You must understand that Shevardnadze
is not the kind of terrorist target that bombs are used
against. Top people are not killed like that. It was all
done in an amateurish way. If I had planned a coup
d'etat, Shevardnadze would not now be able to appear
on TV and you and I would be talking in Georgia. But
nobody gave me or my security service that mission.
{Gamov] So what did happen in Tbilisi 29 August?
{Giorgadze} Unul it happened I had been out of Georgia
for almost a month — first | was on an official visit

abroad, then I was on leave. On 29 August I was in
Moscow. When I heard about the act of terrorism, |

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

flew to Tbilisi immediately. The scene of the attack was
examined without me, so I cannot say what happened.

But I think that the act of terrorism was possible
because the head of state’s personal security directorate
— which was removed from the Security Service and
made directly subordinate to Shevardnadze — failed to
provide elementary security for a routine event. The act
of terrorism took plxce in a closed area that was the
responsibility of the security directorate — the Security
Service was not allowed access. Could it have physically
prevented what happened? Of course not.

{Gamov] You are being accused of knowing about the
planned act of terrorism and of taking no action.

{Giorgadze] Yes, we did have information to that effect.
Our city directorate had been working on one group
{razrabatyvalo odnu gruppu]. Shevardnadze was kept
regularly informed. We proposed a whole series of mea-
sures and pointed out specific areas which needed rein-
forcement from the standpoint of safeguarding security.
We also drew attention to the location where the terror-
ist act subsequently took place. We did everything in
our power.

{Gamov] But the terrorist attack still happened...

{Giorgadze] I'm now starting to incline toward the view
that the conditions for it were artificially created by
Shevardnadze himself. | don’t mean to say that he sat
down and thought about how to bomb himself or what
to make it look like. But the way it was done, the
overall context, and everything that then followed makes
me think that it was not done without some kind of
assistance — put it that way.

Look at what happened. The car carrying the explosive
was parked in a secure area, and the bomb went
off in an area where large numbers of people were
present. But there were no corpses, only a few scratches
from flying glass. And serious observers have said that
the scratches on Shevardnadze’s forehead sometimes
changed position.

[Gamov] Did you meet with Eduard Amvrosiyevich
before he fired you?

[Giorgadze] Yes, he summoned me 2 September and
said that he had no option but to dismiss me. Clearly,
the decision had already been made to pin this terrorist
attack on Giorgadze. I met with all the special subunits
and said that our opponents and enemies were waiting
for some kind of action from us and that this must not
be allowed to happen. I took my dismissal very quietly
and calmly and left Georgia a few days later to provide
for my family.

{Gamov] Did you leave without let or hindrance?

is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

{Giorgadze] MVD [Ministry of Internal Affairs] staffers
circled around my home. But I constantly had my
armed subunits with me. If the MVD men had tried
a provocation, they would have received a response.
Officially nobody tried to stop me.

{Gamov] Are your armed subunits with you now?
{Giorgadze} The armed subunits are in place.
[Gamov] Where exactly?

|Giorgadze| In Georgia, of course.

|Gamov] Just why, as you put it, did they decide to
“pin” the act of terrorism on Giorgadze in particular?
You seemed to be on very good terms with the head of
State...

{Giorgadze] | was not a supporter of his, and he knew
it. Everyone in Georgia knew it. Moreover, I and my
service were in his way. How exactly? Shevardnadze is
heading for elections. The Prosecutor's Office is in his
pocket, the MVD is made up of bandits and is therefore
controllable. But the Security Service stuck in his craw.
It was not openly opposed to him, but it did not support
him when he wanted to circumvent and violate laws and
existing norms. In other words, we could not be used
as a lever in the political struggle.

He was also scared of something else — our service
could expose the falsifications that he would have to
resort to during the elections. Now he has no obstacles
in his way.

I assess the accusations from Tbilisi as Shevardnadze’s
reaction to my open letter, published in the Russian
press, where I accuse him of very serious crimes against
his own people — at the very least of promoting
ethnic cleansing in Georgia, of machinations running
into millions, of arms smuggling from Georgia to Iran....

And, I think, he needed this speculation — accusations
and arrests will tie up certain parties. They even reached
agreement to the point where another presidential can-
didate — Patiashvili — was said to have been living on
the same landing as my father Panteleymon Giorgadze,
leader of the United Communist Party and a presiden-
tral candidate. The neighbors are said to have met and
decided to get rid of Shevardnadze ...

{Gamov} ...To seize the airport, tne telegraph office, and
other important installations, and to set up a Committee
of National Salvation...

|Giorgadze|] My father did not have and does not have
any particular view about me, from the standpoint of
my work. If we had set ourselves the task of seizing the
airport or anything else.... There was no force in Georgia
— in terms either of numbers, equipment, or training —

CAUCASUS 87

that could have stood in the way of the Security Service
if such actions had been planned. If we had toyed even
for a secend with the idea of reviving the civil war,
my team would have been equal to the task. All these
accusations are just crazy. Some kind of theater of the
absurd. And it is hard to comment on them.

[Gamov] Moreover, you are being accused of in-
volvement in the acts of terrorism which led to the
deaths of Georgiy Chanturia, chairman of the Na-
tional Democratic Party; Soliko Khabeishvili [name as
transliterated], president of the Shevardnadze Founda-
tion; Deputy Interior Minister Georgiy Gulua....

[Giorgadze] ...Keep going — Kirov, Kennedy, Olof
Palme.... I can’t say any more.

{Gamov] Even so....

[Giorgadze}] You wouldn't believe it, but Georgia today
is Starting to smack of the thirties-style processes
and trials. And I would like to draw the attention
of human-rights campaigners to this. Everyone has
suddenly admitted overnight that they prepared coups
and organized acts of terrorism. Because psychotropic
methods and inhuman torture are being used during
interrogation. All this “evidence” which Shevardnadze
and people in his entourage are using is downright
farcical.

{Gamov} Can you say who in Georgia way has an
interest in seeing Shevardnadze go?

{Giorgadze] So many people do that it would be hard
to pick anyone out. But then, before coming to any
conclusions, we need a serious investigation, not just
propaganda ploys.

{Gamov] According to information from Thilisi, Igor
Giorgadze has been linked with anti-Yeltsin forces in
Russia.

{Giorgadzej I can assure you that Shevardnadze too will
soon Start speculating on the “Russian trail." My official
contacts in Russia were always in the public eye, and |
had a good professional and personal relationship with
the leaders of the Russian special services. If they are
to be considered “anti- Yeltsin forces”....

[Gamov] There was a rumor that they want to offer you
a top post at the Russian Defense Ministry.

[Giorgadze] I would consider it an honor if my col-
leagues were to consider it possible for me to work
there. But since Shevardnadze has made me an outlaw,
I will not allow them to even suggest it.

{Gamov] Are you currently maintaining any contact
with the Russian special services?

is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

88 CAUCASUS

{Giorgadze] There is no such contact, nor could there
be. And not because somebody does or does not want
to help me. Following Shevardnadze’s official statement
on television that he was appealing to the Russian
authorities to hand me over, I do not plan to put the
Russians in an awkward position. And not only the
Russians. | am very well aware of the countries with
which Georgia has agreements on legal mutual aid, and I
have no intention of going to those CIS countries where
the agreements are in force. So Shevardnadze should not
worry — Russia will not have to hand me over, because
I won't be in Russia.

{Gamov] Where will you be?

[Giorgadze] I'd rather not say. But I am definitely not
planning to run away from Georgia. Today there is
no place for me there. But Georgia will not always

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

be Shevardnadze’s. And I will try to bring that day
nearer. But I plan to use only methods that will not
be condemned by future generations. I do not intend to
take up arms.

I feel very sad about Georgia. It is feudally fragmented
today, producing absolutely nothing, begging with hand
outstretched from all and sundry, it has totally lost all
morality, and it lacks any kind of national idea. In other
words, it is a deadlocked situation. And it will get even
worse. As long as Shevardnadze is in the Transcaucasus
there will continue to be a seat of tension there.

{Gamov] You are being hunted. Aren't you frightened
for your life?

{Giorgadze] This is nothing new for me. Although it is
something you must not get used to....

Latvian ‘Right-Wing Extremist’ To Stand Trial in
Hamburg

AU0910201895 Hamburg BILD AM SONNTAG
in German 8 Oct 95 p2

{Report by Helmut Boeger: “Latvian Election Victor
Must Face Trial in Germany"]

[FBIS Translated Text] The German-Latvian right-wing
extremist Joachim Siegerist (48), whose "Pro Latvia”
party won 16 seats in the Latvian parliamentary elec-
tions last week and will form part of the government,
must face a court in Germany. BILD AM SONNTAG
learned that Klaus Wille, presiding judge in the tiny
Sixth Penal Chamber of the Hamburg Land Court, wants
to put Siegeris on trial before the end of the year for
“instigating racial hatred.”

When Latvia received its independence from the So-
viet Union, Siegerist was granted Latvian citizenship
because his father was also born in the Baltic region.
He subsequently founded “Pro Latvia” and gained a seat
in parliament. In April 1994, he was sentenced to 18-
months imprisonment without remission in Hamburg for
describing gypsies as an “evil, criminal bunch who breed
like rabbits.” Siegerist: “I have not taken back a single
word.” He says that he will travel to Germany to face
trial.

Whichever way the trial ends, Siegerist wants to run for
president of Latvia next year, even though he speaks
the difficult language poorly, which prevented him from
taking up his parliamentary seat personally. “I will win
a majority,” says Siegerist.

Until then, with his right-left coalition of extremists,
he wants to govern poor Latvia together with former
communists, whom he described as “swine, thugs, and
gangsters” before the elections.

Lithuania
Gylys Links Baltic Security to European Security

LD1010191095 Vilnius Radio Vilnius Network
in Lithuanian 1415 GMT 10 Oct 95

{FBIS Translated Excerpt] Lithuanian Foreign Affairs
Minister Povilas Gylys held a news conference at the
Foreign Affairs Ministry today. Here is our correspon-
dent Vincenta Dapkiene with the details.

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

[Dapkiene] The foreign affairs minister presented
Lithuania’s viewpoint on problems of security, its wish
to integrate into Europe, and its concern caused by the
processes taking place in the neighborhood, especially
in the East. He voiced his concern with regard to the
publications on the basic principles of the Russian
defense doctrine prepared by the headquarters of the
Russian Armed Forces, which foresees that Russia will
immediately deploy its troops in Latvia, Lithuania, and
Estonia if the above countries were accepted in NATO.
[passage omitted}

[Begin Gylys recording] Lithuania cannot set up an
individual, separate security system. This is a common
matter for all European countries. [passage omitted]
This government is doing everything in order to avoid
a new division of Europe into confronting blocs. To
my mind, Mr. Landsbergis, does not see any sense in
pursuing such a policy. In other words, he automatically
goes for the so-called black scenario. We cannot deny
that events may take a turn in any direction, therefore,
various scenarios are possible.

However, we must work toward the good scenario,
that is to work in order to reduce the possibility of
confrontation on the continent. The factor of wisdom
and stability urged us to conclude agreements with
its neighbors without much delay concerning the state
borders and ethnic minorities. [passage omitted]

It is clear that Europe will not be able to achieve its
own stability without stability in the Baltic region. This
is a matter of pragmatism. On the other hand, there is
also a certain moral debt. Mr. Kohl (?said this) about
five days ago when he spoke to the Baltic states about
moral debt. And the best way to pay it back is to support
Lithuania's statehood. The Baltic states want to develop
a market economy and a democratic political system.
{end recording]

U.S. Support Welcomed

WS/ 110094395 Tallinn BNS in English
1359 GMT 10 Oct 95

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] Vilnius, Oct 10, BNS — The
Lithuanian minister of foreign affairs Povilas Gylys
welcomed Washington's guarantee of political support
for the sovereignty of the Baltic countries.

At a press conference on Tuesday [10 October], he
commented on a statement by Nicholas Burns, the U.S.
Department of State press spokesman, in which the
U.S. expressed its concern with the security, territorial
integrity and sovereignty of the Baltic countries.

“Any note by anyone in Russia that somehow Russia
has the right to station its troops or to station nuclear

This report may contain copyrighted maternal. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-196
11 October 1995

weapons outside the borders of Russia is irresponsible
and inconsistent with international law," the U.S. State
Department official said. He expressed his criticism
of a draft of Russia’s new military doctrine which
appeared recently in the Russian press. This draft
proposal envisions an invasion of the Baltic states, were
they to become members of NATO.

Alfonsas Eidintas, the Lithuanian ambassador in Wash-
ington, requested the U.S. state department last week
that it publicly express its position on the Russian
threats.

In a transcript of a press briefing received Tuesday by
the Lithuanian foreign ministry from Washington, Burns
recalls the words of American vice-president Al Gore
during his visit to Tallinn in March, when he said that
“the security of Lithuania, Latvia and Estonia is of direct
and material interest to the United States.”

BALTIC STATES 91

In the opinion of foreign minister Povilas Gylys, the
statement of the state department spokesman testifies to
the political vested interest of the U.S. in Baltic security.

“Europe cannot be stable without stability in the Baltic
region —- this is clear to everyone," Gylys said Tuesday.

The minister also mentioned the West's "moral debt" to
the Baltic countries and welcomed the pronouncements
of Helmut Kohl, the German chancellor on this theme.
“It is very touching that Kohl spoke of a moral debt of
the West to the Baltic countries. The best way to repay
this debt is by supporting Lithuanian sovereignty and
the country’s desire to develop a market economy and
a democratic political system," the Lithuanian foreign
minister said.
